Rhapsody is not particularly concerned with the 
minutiae of all the bits and pieces your 
character is carrying. You don’t need to track 
your rations, or your ammunition, or whether or 
not you have a bedroll, camping tent and 50ft of 
rope when traipsing through some wilderness. 
And the less said about encumbrance rules, the 
better. Instead, Dead Metal Rhapsody assumes 
you have all the day-to-day trappings you need 
to get by, such as clothes, a phone, a wallet, 
food, drink and whatever else you need to make 
it through. For bigger and more important stuff, 
though, you’ll use the Signature Gear system, 
and for narrative, character-driven personal 
effects, you’ll use Trinkets. Finally, currency is 
not tracked on a coin-by-coin basis; instead, 
your relative level of affluence is shown by your 
level of Drachma, indicating what you can 
(roughly) afford to buy. This is all designed to 
keep things fast and loose- nothing slows the 
action down more than when all the players 
stop to pore over lists of umpteen items of gear, 
searching for whether they have a ‘crankshaft of 
mechanistry’, or a ‘knob of knobbing’, or a ‘10ft 
pole’ or some shit.  
 
Signature Gear 
Signature Gear represents important and 
impactful objects that define your 
Necromusician’s role and personality within the 
band. All Necromusicians start off with three 
pieces of Signature Gear, which can be anything 
they choose. As a rule of thumb, the following 
characteristics separate Signature Gear from 
Trinkets and day-to-day items: 
 
1. Signature Gear is Impactful: Having 
half a can of beer in your back pocket 
won’t have much of an impact on the 
game; having a demonically-possessed 
chihuahua on a leash probably will. 
Signature Gear should have an 
immediate and obvious effect on the 
game, whether as a deadly weapon, a 
useful tool or a volatile magical 
artefact. 
 
2. Signature Gear is Unique: Boring, 
mundane or otherwise unspecialised 
items aren’t really Signature Gear 
material. For example, a set of tools 
isn’t Signature Gear- but a Music Tech’s 
Power Tool Crate ™ absolutely is! Try to 
refine your Signature Gear to make it 
stand out. Any Tom, Dick or Harry can 
be holding a screwdriver; only you can 
wield the Screwdriver of Gargalmoth, 
Bone Devil of Metal Fixtures and 
Affordable Shelving! 
 
3. Signature Gear is Personal: Your 
Necromusician’s Signature Gear should 
say something about them and the 
talents they bring to the party. Perhaps 
you’re the petrol head of the band, 
driving a souped-up boneshaker and 
never without your heavy leather biker 
jacket and grease-stained toolbox. 
Perhaps you’re a roadie, lugging around 
a heavy-duty amp stack, bedecked in 
cables and wires, looking for someone 
to punch with your Star Coal 
Knuckledusters. Or perhaps you’re a 
depraved black magic scholar, wearing 
complex eyepieces formed of 
interlocking lens as you read your texts 
of profane lore by the light of a Lantern 
of Souls. Try to make your Signature 
Gear reflect your personality and 
character traits; it really helps to flesh 
them out! 
 
Choose THREE pieces of Signature Gear to 
begin play with. As always, your GM has the final 
say on what’s appropriate. During play, your 
Signature Gear can be used to give you 
Advantages in certain Tests, where appropriate, 
as well as having other mechanical uses. These 
uses are only limited by your creativity (and the 
GM’s quick thinking!). 
 
Trinkets 
Unlike Signature Gear, Trinkets are non-
impactful doodads that are much more about 
roleplaying opportunities than they are about 
mechanical advantage. They are the little curios 
that people often carry that say a lot about their 
personalities, such as jewellery, watches, hair 
extensions and ever-burning crystals from the 
Tears of Lacedaemon. 
 
Your Necromusician can choose AS MANY 
Trinkets as it makes sense for them to be able to 
carry. These Trinkets cannot have a significant 
mechanical impact on the game, but are instead 
intended to provide little sparks of character 
and personality. After all, you can tell a lot about 
a lady by the contents of her purse… especially 
if it contains steel guitar strings, a Cannibal 
Corpse CD and a necklace made of teeth! 


 
 
Examples of Signature Gear & Trinkets 
 
Signature Gear 
Weapons 
- 
Sulphur Pistol: a brutal snub-nosed handgun laced with foul-smelling brimstone and incense. 
- 
Dirk of Exsanguination: a knife made of demon’s tooth that causes profuse and noisy bleeding. 
- 
Hell’s Angels Shotgun: a double-barrelled sawn-off that used to belong to a biker gang. 
- 
Rusty but Trusty Axe: it’s an axe. There are many like it. But this rusty bastard is mine. 
 
Clothing 
- 
Crusader Regalia: black-lacquered heavy plate armour designed in baroque, fashionable style. 
- 
Bone-Laced Flak Jacket: combat fatigues reinforced with interlocking bony weave. 
- 
Glowing HazMat Suit: white plastic, radiation shielding and a warm, comforting glow. Lovely! 
- 
Patched War Vest: a denim jacket with band patches, each of which is a living portrait. 
 
Black Magic Artefacts 
- 
Key of Moloch: a frozen key that opens any lock- as long as the lock in question is also frozen. 
- 
Tome of Forbidden Lore: a complex grimoire of occult mysteries, spells and devilish secrets. 
- 
Heart on My Sleeve: you wear your undead heart on your sleeve. Literally. With a little pin. 
- 
Shredder’s Coronet: a crown that increases the speed at which you can wank your guitar. 
 
Pets 
- 
Flesh Pile: an affectionate little pile of stitched offal and eyeballs. He loves you lots. 
- 
Snarky Elitist Gargoyle: a stone devil that reminds you why metal music was a mistake. 
- 
Necromagical Automaton: a cyborg fusion of meat and steel that carries your burdens. And you. 
- 
Scruffy but Loyal Dog: just a very loyal dog. You don’t always have to improve on perfection. 
 
Tools and Equipment 
- 
Music Tech’s Bag of Tricks: all the gear you’ll need to ensure sound at a show runs smoothly. 
- 
Pyrotechnician’s Satchel: WARNING- MAY SPONTANEOUSLY COMBUST. 
- 
Stitcher’s Supplies: for the enterprising doctor who needs to make skin constructs in a hurry. 
- 
Bottle of ‘Medicine’: hey, we all have our vices. Don’t judge me, and I won’t judge you. 
 
Trinkets 
 
- 
A skull belonging to a particularly 
troublesome foe. 
- 
An old LP long since out of print that 
you’ve never played to anyone. 
- 
A studded leather wristband stained 
with dried blood. 
- 
A locked diary bound in demon hide. 
- 
A pair of skinny jeans you have grown 
too much to fit into anymore. 
- 
An old memento of your university 
house you keep in a locket. 
- 
A ring set with a glowing red stone. 
- 
A yellowing setlist from a gig you caught 
several years ago. 
- 
A guitar pick made out of one of your 
teeth. 
- 
A chipped tankard you ‘borrowed’ from 
your favourite bar. 
- 
A broken clock set to two minutes to 
midnight. 
- 
A scrapbook you pour over when your 
life gets lonely. 
- 
A busted-up mobile phone from 1995. 
- 
A crackling Walkman and scratched CD. 


 
 
Drachma 
Drachma are the coins of the Labyrinth, roughly 
octagonal chips of burnished metal that feel 
faintly warm to the touch. No-one is certain 
where they came from, only that they surface in 
the wreckage and detritus like bodies bobbing 
slowly to shore. Regardless of their origin, they 
are accepted by the dead and the living alike as 
all other forms of currency seem to rust and rot 
when taken below the surface. 
 
Unlike other tabletop RPGs, Drachma are not 
counted on a coin-by-coin basis. You don’t need 
to know how many you have at any given 
moment, and items don’t have a hard-and-fast 
price tag. Instead, we abstract both of these 
things. Your Drachma is measured as a ‘Level’ 
and the cost of items and services is given as an 
‘Expense’. The Level of Drachma you have 
indicates your relative affluence and purchasing 
power; the Expense of an item indicates just 
how rare and costly it usually is to acquire. 
 
There are five Levels of Drachma, which are as 
follows: 
 
- 
Skint: A Necromusician who’s Skint is 
barely making ends meet, living from gig 
to gig on a heady mix of pot noodles, 
friends’ mattresses and cheap beer. A 
Skint Necromusician can only afford 
the most basic of necessities and a 
pretty barebones lifestyle; you likely live 
in a crummy bedsit or sofa-surf week to 
week, and have to take odd jobs to fill 
out the income when the band shit 
doesn’t cut it. A Skint Necromusician 
can only regularly afford Items with an 
Expense of ‘Cheap’ or less. They can 
afford up to three ‘Average’ items and 
only one ‘Costly’ item per Session. They 
cannot afford ‘Expensive’ or ‘Luxury’ 
items at all! 
 
- 
Getting By: A Necromusician who’s 
Getting By has enough of an income to 
afford their own place and get by well 
enough, minus a few scrapes here and 
there. They make enough to live 
comfortably, buy decent food and have 
some hobbies and pastimes on the 
side; they likely live in a small 
apartment or share a house 
somewhere, and earn enough money 
from their band that only the occasional 
side hustle is necessary. A Getting By 
Necromusician can regularly afford 
items with an Expense of ‘Cheap’ or 
‘Average’. They can afford up to three 
‘Costly’ items and only one ‘Expensive’ 
item per Session. They cannot afford 
‘Luxury’ items at all! 
 
- 
Well-Off: A Necromusician who’s Well-
Off is doing pretty damn good, having 
enough juice to pay their way through 
the finer things in a comfortable life. 
They can afford to indulge some 
expensive tastes and a handful of 
luxuries; they likely live in a house of 
their own, or a quality apartment in one 
of the nicer districts of Skin City. Side 
jobs? Forget that shit! A Well-Off 
Necromusician can regularly afford 
items with an Expense of ‘Cheap’, 
‘Average’ or ‘Costly’. They can afford up 
to three ‘Expensive’ items and only one 
‘Luxury’ item per Session. 
 
- 
Rich: A Necromusician who’s Rich has 
made it to the big leagues, baby. They 
have cash in hand, and enough reckless 
self-assurance to be able to spend it. 
They can get into exclusive clubs, bribe 
their way past bouncers, cash in on 
pricey instruments and get explosively 
high with the A-Listers when they feel 
like going to town. They likely live in a 
luxury home, or maybe have multiple 
apartments they flit between. A Rich 
Necromusician can regularly afford 
items with an Expense of ‘Cheap’, 
‘Average’, ‘Costly’ and ‘Expensive’. They 
can afford up to three ‘Luxury’ items per 
Session. 
 
- 
Stupid Rich: A Necromusician who’s 
Stupid Rich has more cash than sense. 
They have multiple homes, designer 
wardrobes, whole rooms full of shiny 
cars and guitars and… profitable 
investments (yuck). Their homes are 
luxurious and more than a little showy. 
A Stupid Rich Necromusician can 
regularly afford any items they want, 
including ‘Luxury’ items. 
 
All Necromusicians start off with a Drachma 
Level of Skint. You gotta start from the bottom 
and work your way up! Later in this book, we’ll 
describe how purchasing new items works and 
how you can make your Level go up, as well as 
exactly what each category of Expense means. 


 
 
Bang Your Head (Until You’re Dead) 
 
Seven people. Seven. Fucking. People. The look on Frank’s 
rotting mug could have struck a man blind as he twisted the 
wires to his mic. The club was near empty, a far cry from a 
typical night out in Skin City- there weren’t even any drunks 
or drifters or dead-heads, save for a bored-looking couple 
downing whiskey at the bar. That fucking promoter. Frank could 
picture his slimy grin as he pocketed their hard-earned cash. 
As he promised them big before slinking off down an alleyway 
like a rat in suede shoes. Fuck. The mic cracked as Frank’s grip 
got white-knuckled. A feedback buzz filled the air. 
 
“You good, mate?” The drummer, Liza, leaned over her stool. 
Frank sighed, looked out over the graveyard of a dancefloor. 
Liza caught his eye, and nodded, low and slow. “Don’t worry 
‘bout that. Once the music starts, we’ll fill up. Count on it.” 
She sat back, started to tune her snare. The sound guy raised 
a lazy thumbs-up and stifled a yawn. Frank swallowed. Took a 
deep breath. 
 
Ten seconds later, the mic cable was round the sound guy’s 
neck. Least he looked a bit like that fucking promoter. And, to 
Frank’s mouldering brain, that was good enough. 
 
 


 
 
Step Seven- Personality 
 
At this Step, all the mechanical details of your 
Necromusician are done. You have an Infernal 
Choir, you’ve set your Attributes and 
Secondaries, you’ve chosen some Edges and 
some Necromagical Powers, and you’ve got a 
sense of what gear you’re carrying and how 
much cash money you have. But roleplaying 
characters are far more than just big piles of 
numbers and stats. They have rich personalities, 
backstories and quirks that simply can’t be 
quantified by a +1 modifier or a -2D6 to being 
likeable. At this Step, it’s time to put those last 
few personal details into your Necromusician 
and really bring them to (un)life. 
 
Let’s start with the basics. The first thing you’ll 
need to decide on is your Necromusician’s 
name. This can be their real name, a stage 
name, or some combination of the two. Your 
character’s name is entirely up to you, and can 
be as mundane or as magical as you like. 
 
Next, you’ll need to decide on your character’s 
gender and appearance. Dead Metal Rhapsody 
is an inclusive and welcoming game that 
welcomes people of all genders, ethnicities and 
backgrounds. As such, you are welcome to 
choose any gender that best fits your 
Necromusician, as well as any ethnicity that 
best describes how they identify. There are lots 
of valid things to be angry about in a world on 
the brink of collapse- someone’s identity is not 
fucking one of them. Be welcoming, be inclusive 
and be good people. And let other people be 
who they are. After all, the spirit of heavy metal 
is joining together as part of a rebel community. 
Horns up, headbang, and fuck bigotry in all its 
forms. You do you. 
 
In terms of appearance, the only limit is that 
your Necromusician is, well, undead. There are 
ways and means to disguise this fact, but there 
are always tell-tale signs shining through. 
Perhaps you’re quite literally a walking corpse, 
rotting flesh sloughing off your bones. Maybe 
you’re an ethereal spectre, a ghost of white gas 
or mist, or perhaps you’re a skeletal, leather-
clad hellraiser with a polished yellow skull. 
Maybe you’re really close to passing for human, 
but you lack breath or a heartbeat, or perhaps 
you’re something stranger entirely. Again, the 
final choice is yours. 
 
For your Necromusician’s appearance, we’d 
recommend consulting your Infernal Choir’s 
description and seeing what members of that 
Choir typically end up looking like. This can act 
as useful inspiration if you’re stuck in a rut. 
Otherwise, talk to your fellow players and your 
GM- they might well be able to help you out! 
 
 
There’s a lot of different people down here. 
London was full of folks, from East End lads, to tattooed 
northerners, to Chinese expats, to European backpackers, 
to African tourists, to… Well, you get the picture. And 
now that the city is all fucked up, the diversity’s only 
gotten bigger. Those African tourists? I saw one the 
other day who was twelve feet tall with grafted muscles 
like Frankenstein’s monster. Those East End lads? I saw 
one with jaws like a locust and a rotting arm made of old 
bones and diseased skin. My point is, there ain’t two 
people who look alike down here. Why should you be any 
different? Embrace the chaos. After all, when’s the last 
time you got a chance to resculpt your body? Aside from 
shit plastic surgery and a half-assed tattoo? 
 
After you’ve decided on what your 
Necromusician looks like, jot down a few 
keywords to describe them- just so you have a 
potted description ready to go if you need it. 
Also think about what they typically wear, what 
they sound like and the first impression they 
leave on people. You don’t need to go into a 
tonne of detail- just enough to get yourself 
invested and provide interesting hooks for the 
other players and the GM. 
 
Once you’ve got these details nailed down, it’s 
time to flesh out your Necromusician’s 
personality, i.e. who they are under all that 
make-up, leather and dead flesh! 
 
 
Born to die, ‘cause dying is easy. 
 
Born to be dead, ‘cause living is hard. 
 
Born to rock, ‘cause working is boring. 
 
Born to burn out, like the fire in my 
heart. 
 
 


 
 
In order to get a sense of your Necromusician’s 
personality, try to answer the following eight 
questions with a short sentence or two. You 
don’t have to be super-detailed or write pages 
and pages of backstory (but if you want to, that’s 
cool too!). This is more to just get an overview of 
what your character is like, to let the GM and the 
other players work off of you to tell interesting 
and engaging stories. If anything, leaving some 
bits and pieces vague can also be fun. That way, 
you’ve got some free plot hooks for your GM to 
use, helping draw you and your character into 
the story. 
 
The eight Personality Questions are as follows: 
 
a. What was your old life like, 
before you died? 
 
b. How did you die and why did 
you take the Archdevil’s deal? 
 
c. What do you love most? 
 
d. What do you stand for? 
 
e. What is your greatest failing? 
 
f. 
What do you want most out of 
your unlife? 
 
g. Who are your closest friends? 
 
h. Who are your deadliest 
enemies? 
 
Once you’ve answered these questions, you’re 
done. That’s right- your Necromusician is good 
to go! Prepare to dive headfirst into the world of 
the Pit and the Labyrinth… Your undead 
rockstar’s heavy metal legacy begins right here, 
right now! 
 
This next bit is the profile of a bandmate I 
once had when I was singing for Living Corrosion- fucking 
awesome grind band from Brixton. Three-piece, raw as 
hell, tore down a bar on their first gig and set a roadie on 
fire when the management refused to pay. The person in 
question’s from the States. Love ‘em  to bits, even 
though each time we meet they’ve got a bit more metal 
on their bones and a bit less skin. I’ve left out the 
details of their personality, but I’m sure you can imagine. 
Fill in the gaps yourself, eh? The mechanics are all there 
though- this is what your Necromusician should eventually 
look like. 
EXAMPLE NECROMUSICIAN 
EFFIE “COLD KNIVES” CALLIOU 
Bassist in the Grind Band “Living Corrosion” 
 
Age: 29 
Gender: Non-Binary 
Ethnicity: Native American (Navajo) 
 
Infernal Choir: Engine of Destruction 
 
Attributes 
 
Guts 
Brains 
Spine 
3 
1 
2 
 
Secondaries 
 
• 
Armour: Physical (5+) 
• 
Defence: 4+ 
• 
Necromagic: 3 
• 
Speed: Fast 
• 
Will: 3 
• 
Wounds: 5 
 
Edges 
- 
Hunting in the wilderness 
- 
Conducting surgical procedures and 
gruesome operations 
- 
Crime scene investigation and tracking 
- 
Skateboarding tricks and badassery 
 
Necromagical Powers 
- 
Machine Curse (Minor): cause a 
mechanical device to rust and decay. 
- 
Like Clockwork (Major): fuse cogs into 
a victim’s spine and puppeteer them. 
- 
Time Will Die (Metal): rapidly 
accelerate the flow of time within a 
vortex of frozen dust. 
 
Instrument and Signature Gear 
- 
Thunderhorse (Instrument): a bass 
guitar that incorporates a crackling 
tesla coil and copper capacitor. 
- 
Surgeon’s Toolkit: a bag of pristine 
surgical tools and chemical vials. 
- 
Thrice-Cursed Fleshclock: a cuckoo 
clock of dead flesh possessed by three 
demons. 
- 
Skateboard: a top-shelf skateboard. 
 
- 
Trinkets: old Navajo headdress, 
dreamcatcher, Morbid Angel tour t-
shirt, old industry punchcard. 
 
- 
Drachma Level: Skint 


 
 
War Ensemble 
Combat: How to Fight the Fiends of the Labyrinth 
 
Fighting the “Good” Fight 
 
You already know the Core Mechanic of Dead 
Metal Rhapsody. In most circumstances, you 
don’t need to know more than that. Your 
Necromusician will encounter an obstacle and 
make a Test to try and overcome it, whether that 
obstacle is physical, mental, magical or 
personal. But, when shit really hits the fan and a 
brawl breaks out, there are a few more rules 
you’ll need to know. Combat works a little 
differently than just making a few Tests. This 
section will explain how you can fight the “good” 
fight against the horrifying denizens of the 
Labyrinth. 
 
How Does Combat Start? 
 
When a fight breaks out, for whatever reason, 
the GM will announce that you’re now in 
Combat. This could be the result of a drunken 
brawl spilling out of an old town bar, a demonic 
ambush in the shadows deep below Skin City, or 
a desperate assault on an insect-ridden undead 
forge to rescue a kidnapped bandmate. 
Basically, when it becomes important to know 
who’s swinging, at what and when, that’s when 
the GM should call for Combat. 
 
Unlike most other TTRPGs, Dead Metal 
Rhapsody does not make use of a Combat Grid. 
Instead, Combat takes place entirely within the 
theatre of the mind, and, as a result, is quite 
heavily abstracted. Whereas other games may 
care about the exact metre-by-metre position of 
a character, or how many enemies are within 
15ft of an exploding sphere, Dead Metal 
Rhapsody is more concerned with making 
Combat fast, flavourful, functional and deadly. 
That said, there’s still a framework to follow. 
Can’t let it get too abstract, after all- otherwise, 
there’s not much of a game to play! 
 
 
 
 
The Combat Round 
 
Combat is played out in Rounds, with the first 
Round beginning as soon as the GM calls for 
Combat. Each Round represents approximately 
half a minute, or thirty seconds, of frantic 
action, though this is more of a guideline than a 
hard-and-fast rule. During a Round of Combat, 
players and NPCs involved in the Combat will 
take Turns Moving and Acting, with the GM 
calling for Tests where appropriate to resolve 
difficult or dangerous Actions. Blood will be 
spilled, bones will be broken and, hopefully, 
some of you might make it out alive! 
 
The Combat procedure is as follows: 
 
1. Set the Scene: The GM describes the 
environment that Combat is taking 
place in, as well as the NPCs taking 
part. They describe where the 
Necromusicians find themselves, and 
whether there are any environmental 
hazards or dangers they should be 
aware of. The players can ask the GM 
questions about the environment at this 
stage, to get a better understanding of 
where they stand. 
 
2. Initiative: Players and NPCs in Combat 
are ranked in a Turn Order according to 
their Speed, from Fast to Slow. If a 
player and an NPC have the same 
Speed, the player always ranks above 
the NPC. If two or more players have the 
same Speed, they get to choose their 
ranking order. The Turn Order dictates 
when characters get to Move and Act 
during Combat. 
 
3. Take Turns: Starting with the character 
at the top of the Turn Order, each 
character in the Combat gets to take it 
in turns to Move and/or Act. This is 
described in more detail later. 
 


 
 
4. End of Combat: Combat ends when 
one side has been utterly destroyed, 
has surrendered, or some other 
objective (as determined by the GM) 
has been met. The GM has the final say 
on how and when a Combat ends. 
 
Actions During Combat 
 
On a character’s Turn during Combat, they are 
allowed to Move and/or to Act. A ‘Move’ 
represents any traversal of the environment the 
Combat is taking place in, from simply walking 
from one spot to another to leaping across a 
yawning chasm or diving away from a primed 
grenade. An ‘Act’ represents any course of 
action a character may take when under fire, 
from attacking an enemy with their weapon, to 
using a Necromagical Power, to improvising a 
rune-etched snare to catch a rampaging spider 
demon. Each character may only Move and/or 
Act once on each of their Turns. 
 
When a character decides to Move, they 
describe what they’re attempting to do to the 
GM. Depending on what they’re attempting, the 
GM may call for a Test to see if they can pull it off 
successfully. For example, just charging a 
Zombie Punk wouldn’t require a Test, as it’s 
quite a simple business, but cartwheeling past a 
wall of spinning blades to assail a golem made 
entirely out of skinned faces would require a 
Test to dodge out of harm’s way. If this Test is 
Successful, the character can Move as they 
described. If not, they suffer a consequence at 
the discretion of the GM. Perhaps they got cut by 
the blades, or maybe their attack is now off-
balance and suffers from a Disadvantage. The 
GM has the final say. 
 
When a character decides to Act, they describe 
what they’re attempting to do to the GM. If they 
intend to harm an enemy directly, their Action is 
referred to as an ‘Attack’ and follows a different 
procedure than a normal Action would (see 
below). If they are instead intending to do 
something else, their Action follows the usual 
rules, requiring a Test to Succeed. An Act in 
Combat could be tearing out the supports of an 
overhanging walkway, or barging an ally out of 
the way of an oncoming foe, or casting a spell to 
shield yourself from harm. An Attack, by 
contrast, would be lamping an enemy with your 
guitar, shooting them with a pistol or otherwise 
inflicting some serious harm. The GM again has 
the final say. 
 
Attacks and How They Work 
 
If a character Acts to physically harm an enemy, 
such as by tackling them to the ground, shooting 
at them with a sawn-off shotgun or slicing at 
them with Gor’Manglor, Daemon-Blade of the 
Screaming Abyss, they need to make an Attack. 
In addition, if they intend to mentally harm an 
enemy, such as by poisoning their mind with 
words, driving them insane with a ritual chant or 
persuading them to stand down, they also need 
to make an Attack. 
 
An Attack is a type of Test, but works slightly 
differently than normal. The process for making 
an Attack is as follows: 
 
1. Describe the Attack: The player 
describes what they want their Attack to 
be. 
 
2. Determine Attribute: Physical Attacks 
always use Guts as their Attribute, 
whereas Mental Attacks use Spine. 
Necromagical Powers never require an 
Attack. 
 
3. Edges, Advantages and 
Disadvantages: If the character has 
any Edges that apply to the Attack, or if 
they have any situational Advantages or 
Disadvantages, these are applied now. 
 
4. Roll Dice: The character rolls a number 
of D6 equal to their Attribute that is 
being used for the Attack, +1D6 for 
every Edge or Advantage, minus -1D6 
for every Disadvantage, to a maximum 
of 6D6 or a minimum of 1D6. If at least 
one result is equal to or greater than the 
target’s Defence, the Attack Succeeds. 
If all results are lower than the target’s 
Defence, the Attack Fails. 
 
5. Build Star Power: For every result equal 
to or greater than the target’s Defence 
beyond the first, the character gains 
one Star Power (to a maximum of three). 
Star Power can be spent for Flourishes, 
bonus effects that apply to the Attack’s 
outcome. Attacks can also use Star 
Power to inflict extra Damage, at a rate 
of +1 Damage per Star Power spent. Any 
Star Power not spent is lost. 


 
 
 
6. Determine Outcome and Inflict 
Damage: The GM describes the result 
of the Attack with the help of the player. 
In addition, the target suffers 1 Damage, 
either to their Wounds (for Physical 
Attacks) or to their Will (for Mental 
Attacks). This can be reduced by 
Armour, where applicable. 
 
Step One- Describe the Attack 
 
Just like with a Test, an Attack begins with the 
attacking character describing what they want 
their Attack to be. Remember, an Attack is any 
attempt made to directly harm an opponent- but 
that doesn’t mean an Attack always has to be 
Physical. Attacks can also be Mental, affecting a 
target’s psyche, emotions or loyalties. The 
following are some examples of Physical and 
Mental Attacks, with some short descriptions: 
 
Physical Attacks 
- 
Attempting to hurl a burly Corpse 
Roadie off of a cliff. 
- 
Shooting at a hunkered-down Dead 
Head with a pistol. 
- 
Stabbing an oncoming demon before it 
reaches your stricken friend. 
- 
Punching a man in the face. 
- 
Punching a man in the dick. 
- 
Punching anything, really. 
 
Mental Attacks 
- 
Attempting to coerce a gunman into 
dropping their weapon. 
- 
Undermining the confidence of a brash 
and swaggering mercenary. 
- 
Gaslighting a guy into believing his allies 
have abandoned him. 
- 
Inspiring delusional and erratic 
behaviour. 
- 
Defusing a violent situation. 
- 
Creating a violent situation. 
 
Just as with a Test, an Attack should be 
described in enough detail to get your intentions 
across clearly. Sometimes it’s enough to just say 
“I’m going to shoot the guy”, but if your intent is 
to “pick up the Impling, twirl him around my 
head and then send him flying into his mates 
with a resounding crash”, make sure you explain 
as such before making the Attack! The more 
detail you give, the more the GM will be able to 
ensure your Attack goes the way you want it to. 
Step Two- Determine Attribute 
 
If your Attack is Physical, i.e. you’re attempting 
to inflict some sort of bodily harm, the Attribute 
used for your Attack will be Guts. If your Attack 
is Mental, i.e. you’re attempting to convince, 
coerce, charm or gaslight, the Attribute used for 
your Attack will be Spine. 
 
Necromagical Powers never require an Attack to 
be made in order to use them. Their cost is 
measured in the amount of Necromagic you 
have to spend. A Necromagical Power is always 
used successfully without the need to roll any 
dice- the price you pay is recharging your 
batteries once they’re all burned out! 
 
Step Three- Edges, Advantages and 
Disadvantages 
 
In exactly the same way as a Test, Attacks are 
subject to Edges, Advantages and 
Disadvantages. If your character has any Edges 
that apply to the Attack they’re trying to make, 
you add +1D6 to your roll per Edge. Equally, if 
your environment or equipment offers you an 
Advantage, at the discretion of the GM, you can 
add another +1D6 to your roll per Advantage. 
Finally, if any Disadvantages apply, whether 
from the traits of your target to the layout of the 
combat arena, you must subtract 1D6 from your 
roll per Disadvantage. As with Tests, the 
maximum number of D6s you can roll is 6D6, 
and the minimum number is 1D6. 
 
Step Four- Roll Dice 
 
Roll a number of D6 equal to the Attribute being 
used for the Attack, plus or minus any D6s from 
Edges, Advantages and Disadvantages, to a 
maximum of 6D6 or a minimum of 1D6. Unlike 
Tests, however, you’re not looking to roll results 
of 4+. Instead, you’re looking to roll results equal 
to or higher than the target’s Defence. 
 
For example, if your target has a Defence of 3+, 
you’re looking for at least one of your D6s to 
result in 3 or higher for the Attack to be 
Successful. By contrast, if your target has a 
Defence of 5+, you need at least one 5 or higher. 
The higher a target’s Defence, the harder it is to 
land a blow on them, or to influence them in 
Combat. Determine whether your Attack is 
Successful or a Failure in the same way as you 
would for a Test. 


 
 
Step Five- Build Star Power 
 
If you rolled at least two results equal to or 
higher than your target’s Defence on your 
Attack, not only did you Succeed, but you also 
unlocked your Star Power! Characters can 
generate up to three points of Star Power from a 
Successful Attack, which can be spent to use 
Flourishes and/or to increase the Damage their 
Attack inflicts. You generate one point of Star 
Power, to a maximum of three, for every result 
equal to or higher than your target’s Defence on 
your Attack beyond the first.  
 
You can use the same five Flourishes when 
making an Attack- Badassery, Slickness, 
Loudness, Subtlety and Speed- as you could on 
a normal Test. Again, you as the player get to 
decide how a Flourish affects the Attack. 
Perhaps you use Speed to plough through your 
opponent, lunging quickly towards his allies, or 
maybe you use Loudness to brutally dispatch a 
foe in an almost comical cavalcade of gore. The 
only limit to Flourishes on Attacks is that they 
can’t let you make another Attack for free- no 
slickly slicing through whole hordes of enemies 
in one Act! 
 
Instead of using Star Power for Flourishes as 
part of an Attack, you can spend it to increase 
your Damage. Attacks inflict 1 point of Damage 
baseline. If the Attack is Physical, this Damage 
is inflicted to the target’s Wounds. If the Attack 
is Mental, it is instead inflicted to the target’s 
Will. You can spend Star Power to increase the 
amount of Damage your Attack inflicts by +1 per 
point of Star Power. You may spend Star Power 
to use a Flourish as well as increasing your 
Damage, should you have enough to spend. 
 
Step Six- Determine Outcome and Inflict 
Damage 
 
Depending on whether your Attack Succeeded 
or Failed, the GM now narrates the outcome, 
with a little help from you and your Flourishes. If 
you Succeeded, you achieve what you set out to 
do- congratulations, mate, you won. If you 
Failed, there’s going to be a consequence. 
Perhaps someone gets hurt, you fall short at a 
crucial moment or life simply sucks for a little 
while. The exact details are up to you and your 
GM. 
 
In addition to the Outcome of the Attack, you 
also inflict Damage if your Attack was 
Successful. At baseline, an Attack inflicts 1 
Damage, plus 1 for every point of Star Power 
spent. Subtract the amount of Damage your 
Attack inflicted from the target’s Wounds (for 
Physical Attacks) or from the target’s Will (for 
Mental Attacks). 
 
If a target is reduced to 0 Wounds from an 
Attack, one of two things happens. If they’re an 
NPC, and they’re not of Elite Rank or higher, they 
die. Splat. If they’re a PC, or an NPC of Elite or 
Tyrant Rank, they roll a number of D6 equal to 
their Guts or Rank (plus any Edges, Advantages 
or Disadvantages, at the discretion of the GM). If 
they roll at least one 6, they keep on fighting, but 
must roll again with a Disadvantage if they lose 
any more Wounds (this is cumulative for every 
loss of Wounds beyond the first time they hit 0). 
If they roll no 6s, but at least one 4+, they fall 
unconscious, and are out of commission for the 
rest of the Combat. If they roll nothing better 
than a 1, 2 or 3, then it’s curtains for them- 
Second Death comes, and the Archdevils take 
their due. At the end of Combat, any character 
on 0 Wounds automatically recovers 1 Wound. 
 
If a target is reduced to 0 Will from an Attack, 
one of two things happens. If they’re an NPC, 
and they’re not of Elite Rank or higher, they fall 
under the players’ control. Depending on how 
they lost their last point of Will, this could mean 
they become irrational and erratic, that they are 
now a reluctant ally, or that they’ve completely 
lost their minds. The players get to decide as a 
group how the NPC reacts and acts until the end 
of Combat, keeping in mind that this is a 
roleplaying opportunity, not just a chance to get 
someone to do exactly what they want (although 
that can happen too!). At the end of Combat, the 
GM resumes control of the NPC, who’s 
behaviour has likely been heartily shaken up by 
the ordeal. 
 
If a PC, or NPC of Elite or Tyrant Rank, is reduced 
to 0 Will, they roll a number of D6 equal to their 
Brains or Rank (plus any Edges, Advantages or 
Disadvantages, at the discretion of the GM). If 
they roll at least one 6, they keep their head and 
continue acting as normal, but must roll again 
with a Disadvantage if they lose any more Will 
(this is cumulative for every loss of Will beyond 
the first time they hit 0). If they roll no 6s, but at 
least one 4+, then they are shaken- they fall 
under the GM’s control (for PCs) or the players’ 
control (for NPCs) until the end of their next 
Turn, when they must roll again. If they roll 
nothing better than a 1, 2 or 3, then they’re 


 
 
under the GM’s, or players’, control for the rest 
of the Combat, their minds in upheaval from the 
experience. At the end of Combat, any character 
on 0 Will automatically recovers 1 Will. 
 
Armour 
 
Some characters will have Armour, i.e. a form of 
protection that can shield them from harm. 
There are three types of Armour in Dead Metal 
Rhapsody: Physical, Mental and Magical. When 
a character takes Damage from a source that 
they have Armour against, they roll a number of 
D6s equal to the amount of Damage they have 
taken. For every result that equals or exceeds 
the character’s Armour value, they reduce the 
amount of Damage they take by 1 (to a minimum 
of 0). The following is a reminder of the types of 
Armour and what they protect against: 
 
- 
Physical: Physical Armour protects 
from bodily harm and injury, such as 
gunshots, switchblades, explosions 
and giant fuck-off demon claws. 
 
- 
Mental: Mental Armour protects from 
psychological horrors, such as terrifying 
entities, creeping insanity, isolation and 
the existential crisis of being undead. 
 
- 
Magical: Magical Armour protects 
against sorcery and black magic, such 
as malicious spells, words of power and 
the predations of the Archdevils.  
 
 
Armour is your last line of protection against all the ugly 
fuckers who are gonna want to kill your ass. It’s nice to 
know, sometimes, that there’s a big fuck-off plate of dark 
steel between you and the claws of a Rat Demon, or that 
there’s a warding glyph preventing that Necromagical 
curse from turning your guts inside out. But don’t rely on 
it to keep you alive. The best damage mitigation is not 
getting hit at all. Play it smart, play it cool. Use your 
Move to get out of harm’s way, and fight with your head, 
not just your heart. It’s all well and good doing cool shit, 
but, eventually, some of that cool shit might just come 
and bite you. 
 
Plus, the enemies will have just as much, if not more, 
protection than you- and can be twice as dangerous! If 
you’ve got the choice between fighting one-on-one with a 
Ripper Mech or being twenty yards away… Well, I know 
how I’m avoiding a fucking trip to the doctor’s! 
An Example Round of Combat 
 
Roderika (played by Lara) and Dave Stone 
(played by Alan) have been ambushed in a dive 
bar by a gang of Dead Heads. The GM, Ben, sets 
the scene. The twosome are standing in the 
centre of a dimly-lit taproom, full of smoke, 
tables, barstools and drunken patrons. The bar 
is behind them, covered in bottles, half-empty 
glasses and beer mats. There’s only one way in 
and one way out- a door on the far side of the 
room. The Dead Heads have moved to surround 
them- there’s four of them in total, plus a 
Stitched Together they’re holding on the end of a 
chain. 
 
Now that the scene has been set, it’s time for 
Initiative. Dave Stone has a Speed of Fast, whilst 
Roderika has a Speed of Average. The Dead 
Heads have a Speed of Average, and the 
Stitched Together has a Speed of Slow. The Turn 
Order is as follows: Dave Stone, followed by 
Roderika, followed by the Dead Heads, followed 
by the Stitched Together. 
 
Dave Stone takes his Turn. He uses his Move to 
dive behind the bar, grabbing a handful of beer 
bottles as he goes. The GM rules that this 
doesn’t require a Test- it’s easy enough for Dave 
to fling himself into cover. Dave then decides to 
use one of his Signature Items, a flask of ever-
burning sulphur, to light a bottle of vodka on fire 
like an improvised Molotov before throwing it at 
one of the Dead Heads. He makes an Attack 
against the Dead Head, using his Guts as the 
Attack is Physical. He describes how he wants 
to set the Dead Head ablaze and create a 
distraction for his mates. 
 
The GM informs Dave that the Dead Head has a 
Defence of 3+, meaning Dave will need to roll at 
least one 3+ on his Attack for it to be Successful. 
Dave has a Guts of 3, and thus rolls 3D6. He gets 
results of 1, 3 and 4. A Successful Attack- and 
one Star Power to spend! Dave chooses to use it 
on the Badassery Flourish, inspiring awe in the 
bar patrons as he immolates the Dead Head in a 
huge burst of flame. The GM describes how the 
Dead Head stumbles back, covered in flames. 
The Dead Head takes 1 Damage, reducing his 
Wounds by 1- he’s got no Physical Armour to 
help him out this time! 
 
Now that Dave has Moved and Acted, he passes 
the Turn to Roderika. Now it’s her Turn to fight 
back against the Dead Heads! 


 
 
Combat in a Nutshell 
 
The Combat Process 
1. Set the Scene: The GM describes the environment and the combatants. 
2. Initiative: The Turn Order is set in order of Speed. Ties are broken in favour of the players. 
3. Take Turns: Each character involved in the Combat takes a Turn according to the Turn Order. 
a. Move: A character can Move once on their Turn. Complex moves may require a Test. 
b. Act: A character can Act once on their Turn. Complex actions may require a Test, and 
any attempt to harm an enemy requires an Attack. Necromagical Powers count as an 
Action, but do not require Tests or Attacks. 
4. End of Combat: Characters keep taking Turns until the GM decides that the Combat is over. 
 
 
The Attack 
1. Describe the Attack: The attacker describes what they want their Attack to do. 
2. Determine Attribute: Physical Attacks use Guts, whereas Mental Attacks use Spine. 
3. Edges, Advantages and Disadvantages: Apply all positive modifiers from applicable 
Edges/Advantages and all negative modifiers from applicable Disadvantages. 
4. Roll Dice: Roll a number of D6s equal to Attribute + [Edges + Advantages] – Disadvantages 
(maximum 6D6 and minimum 1D6). At least one result equal to or greater than the target’s 
Defence = Successful Attack. No results equal to or greater than the target’s Defence = Failed 
Attack. 
5. Build Star Power: For every additional result equal to or greater than the target’s Defence beyond 
the first, generate one Star Power (maximum 3). Spend Star Power for Flourishes or for +1 
Damage for every Star Power spent. 
6. Determine Outcome and Inflict Damage: GM describes the outcome of the Attack. The Attack 
inflicts 1 Damage, plus 1 Damage for every Star Power spent on increasing Damage. Physical 
Attacks reduce Wounds; Mental Attacks reduce Will. If the target has Armour appropriate for the 
type of Attack, roll a number of D6 equal to the Damage taken. For every result equal to or greater 
than the target’s Armour, reduce the amount of Damage taken by 1 (to a minimum of 0). 
 
Damage 
Wounds 
NPCs below ‘Elite’ or ‘Tyrant’ Rank: If reduced to 0 Wounds, die. 
PCs and NPCs of ‘Elite’ or ‘Tyrant’ Rank: If reduced to 0 Wounds, roll a number of D6s equal to Guts or 
Rank, plus or minus any Edges, Advantages or Disadvantages. 
- 
At Least One 6: Continue fighting as normal. If required to roll again, suffer a Disadvantage. 
- 
At Least One 4+ but No 6: Fall unconscious. 
- 
No 4+: Die. 
 
Will 
NPCs below ‘Elite’ or ‘Tyrant’ Rank: If reduced to 0 Will, fall under players’ control. 
PCs and NPCs of ‘Elite’ or ‘Tyrant’ Rank: If reduced to 0 Will, roll a number of D6s equal to Brains or Rank, 
plus or minus any Edges, Advantages or Disadvantages. 
- 
At Least One 6: Continue fighting as normal. If required to roll again, suffer a Disadvantage. 
- 
At Least One 4+ but No 6: Fall under players’/GM’s control until end of next Turn. 
- 
No 4+: Fall under players’/GM’s control until end of Combat. 


 
 
Healing, Recovery and Avoiding Second 
Death 
 
 
Phew- that’s a lot of ways to get hurt 
and/or die, huh? Not to mention to go 
irrevocably insane! Fortunately, it takes a lot to 
keep a good Undead down- especially one with 
the dollar to get a good doctor, or, as the case 
may be, a mortician. There’s a lot of ways your 
PCs can recover lost Wounds and Will. Here’s a 
few examples. 
 
Natural Recovery 
 
An Undead ‘heals’ quickly- when you’re 
mostly dead flesh and bones anyway, it turns 
out there’s not a lot you need to do to keep one 
foot out of the grave. 
 
 
As long as they have at least one Wound 
or point of Will left, a Necromusician recovers 
all of their lost Wounds and Will after four hours 
of rest and recovery. This could be ‘sleeping’  in 
a coffin full of comforting grave dirt, getting your 
bones wired back together by a skilful 
Necrosurgeon, or just spending some time with 
your friends, an LP of Cannibal Corpse’s 
greatest hits and a relaxing smoke of pot. 
Basically, whatever your character would 
typically do to wind down when they’re not on 
the job. If you want to compare it to ‘that game’, 
you could call it a ‘Long Rest’, I suppose, but do 
that on your own time. I don’t wanna get sued. 
 
 
If a Necromusician survives a Combat 
or dangerous scenario with 0 Wounds or Will 
remaining, they must receive either medical 
attention or magical healing before they can 
recover naturally. This represents damage that’s 
a bit harder to heal with a soak in the tub, or a 
glass of beer by the fireside- this is the sort of 
stuff you need help with. Once the 
Necromusician has received appropriate help, 
they regain 1 Wound, or 1 Will, as appropriate, 
and then may continue to heal naturally. 
 
Examples of Medical Attention and Magical 
Healing 
- 
Going for a check-up and a once-over at 
the local doctor’s surgery. 
- 
Visiting a mortician’s and getting 
yourself stitched up. 
- 
Patching your own wounds with spare 
flesh and a sewing kit. 
- 
Attending a psychiatry or therapy 
session. 
- 
Using medicinal drugs or psychoactives 
to steady your mind. 
- 
Using Necromagic to restore your vigour 
(if not your good looks!) 
- 
Drawing on a Black Magic Artefact to 
restore the damaged fragments of your 
mind. 
 
A Note on ‘Definitely Not Long Rests’ 
 
In order to recover from four hours’ rest, 
you must do so in a safe and non-stressful 
environment. A secure corner of the Labyrinth 
hemmed in with magical traps would count; an 
active warzone in the frozen wastes of Hell 
definitely wouldn’t. The GM has the final say on 
where PCs can rest and recover- go with 
whatever makes the most sense, and be fair. 
Your character may get off on violence, but 
they’re not going to be able to truly unwind when 
every step could be straight on top of a 
landmine. 
 
 
In addition, characters can only take a 
four-hour rest slot once every 24 hours. I wish I 
didn’t have to do this, I really do, but we’ve all 
had those parties who clear a room in the 
dungeon, set up camp, fully heal, clear the next 
one, and rinse and repeat. That’s boring. That’s 
boring, and it takes the fun out of the game. 
That’s why you can only ‘long rest’ (sorry) once 
every 24 hours. Let there be a little tension, 
yeah? A little drama in the narrative? It’s no fun 
for anyone if your characters just win all the 
time. 
 
First Aid 
 
With the right gear, an enterprising 
Undead can patch themselves up nice and 
quick, Bristol fashion. Sure, giving first aid to a 
literal skeleton can be a challenge, but it’s just a 
different sort of training, just like how 
medicating a ghost can be a bit trickier than 
medicating a zombie unless you know which bit 
of ectoplasm to stick the needle into. 
 
 
A PC with appropriate mundane and/or 
magical medical supplies can attempt to offer 
first aid to a character once every 24 hours. To 
do so, they must make a Brains Test. If they 
succeed, they restore 1 Wound and/or Will (as 
appropriate) to the character, plus one 
additional Wound and/or point of Will for every 
roll of 4+ beyond the first. If they fail, they may 
not attempt another first aid Test until 24 hours 
have passed- there’s nothing more to be done! 
 


 
 
A Note on Second Death, and How to 
Handle the Death of a Player  
 
 
Alright, look. This ain’t that other game, 
at least not to start with. You aren’t gonna be 
immortal gods of destruction, rampaging your 
way through the world without a care until much 
later in your Necromusical careers. Sometimes, 
you’re going to be vulnerable. Sometimes, you’re 
going to fuck up, and be caught off-guard. And, 
sadly, sometimes, your beloved Necromusician 
is going to die. 
 
 
Second Death is utterly terminal. The 
soul is plucked out of the body, and either 
devoured by a hungry Archdevil or bottled for 
sale on the black market as a spectral slave of 
broken dreams and memory. In the former’s 
case, there’s no coming back. The soul is 
annihilated. The machinery of death is broken, 
and there’s nowhere for it to go. In the latter’s 
case, you could come back, but you might not 
want to. Bottling the soul has some… unique 
side-effects on the human psyche. Not much of 
you is left by the point they stuff you into the 
flask. Most go irrevocably insane or dissociate, 
becoming someone else entirely to cope with 
the pain and separation. Perhaps you want to 
GM a story where a bottled soul comes back to 
life- hell, that sounds cool, sign me up- but it’s 
not the norm. Second Death is just that. It’s 
Death. Straight up. No metaphors, allegories or 
theories. Just Death. 
 
 
However, to your players, a character’s 
death will very rarely be just that. One of the 
best parts of RPGs is getting used to a character, 
really bringing out their personality and history, 
exploring how they are as people. We get 
attached, and that’s beautiful, but it fucking 
sucks when things go wrong. Character deaths 
should be handled with dignity. When in doubt, 
follow these tips, GMs and players alike. 
 
“If Death Must Come, Make It a Good Death” 
 
No-one likes to go out like a chump. It’s 
important that characters get to die just as fast 
and hard as they lived. It’s just rubbing salt in the 
wound if your favourite PC dies in a way that 
seems demeaning or underwhelming. As a GM, 
you should give your dying characters a chance 
for a final monologue, act of heroism or just a 
crazy, over-the-top heavy metal exit that people 
won’t soon forget. Imagine the following 
scenarios, for emphasis: 
 
1. Jack Stallion (played by Ryan) is struck 
down by a Gore Fiend, his stomach split 
open and his organs splattered around 
the chamber. In his last moments, he 
grasps his own intestines, which are 
caught in the Gore Fiend’s jaws, and 
pulls the bastard into him. Reaching 
into his chest cavity, Jack pulls out a 
broken rib, and, winking back at his 
lover Lucy Ironbootz, rams the pointy 
end into the Gore Fiend’s blood reactor. 
Jack and the Gore Fiend explode in a 
vortex of pulped flesh and blood, 
allowing his allies to escape and 
destroying the monster utterly. Jack’s 
body disintegrates with an honest-to-
goodness, goddamn smile on his face. 
 
2. Jack Stallion (played by Ryan) gets 
stabbed and fucking dies. 
 
As funny as a random stabbing is in a 
tabletop game, be honest- which of the two 
games would you rather be a part of? The one 
where you get to go out like a badass, or the one 
where the GM makes your character feel like an 
idiot? 
 
“Death Should Be Meaningful, Not Random” 
 
If a character has to die, it should be for 
a good reason. Sure, sometimes the dice will 
betray you, and things won’t go your way- but in 
these circumstances, you need to work with 
your GM and your fellow players to determine 
why that story beat makes sense and how to 
make the resultant death mean something. And, 
to be honest, if a death is just pure unfair 
randomness, it’s OK to use the following 
optional rule: 
- 
Optional Rule: Twist of Fate. After a 
particularly unsatisfying or unfortunate 
death, the GM can save a character’s 
live through circumstances they and the 
player playing that character decide. In 
exchange, the character loses 
something, or changes in a significant 
way, or owes someone- or something- 
dangerous a favour. 
 
“Death Should be an Opportunity” 
 
A character’s death should provoke 
roleplay, reactions and meaningful change in 
the story. You’re not Spinal Tap’s drummer- 
you’re someone who really matters. Work 
together to give character deaths a legacy, and 
explore their impact on the world. 
 
 


 
 
“I once had a friend down this end o’ town. Lived on Old 
Portland Street, ‘im and ‘is daughter- no mum, on 
account of the fact she died in the fall. He wanted me to 
teach ‘is daughter to play the guitar. First off, I said no. 
Didn’t want ‘er mixed up in Necromusic, not with ‘er 
being no more than sixteen.” 
 
The drunk stares into his glass, his single eye bleary and 
bloodshot, limned with stress. He swirls around the 
whiskey, and waits for the residue to settle. The barkeep’s 
started closing up. His bones click on the tiles. 
 
“He kept pressing me, so I did it. I got her playin’, and 
she made the big leagues, not two years later. Played 
lead for the Morticians, and was damn good too. Yeah, she 
was damn good. Too good, as it turned out. Drew the 
wrong sort of attention. Her old man stood up for her. I 
should ‘ave, and all- I should ‘ave been there.” 
 
The drunk raises his glass. 
 
“Here’s to you, Steve. Greatest dad I ever knew. May 
you be in Heaven before the Devil knows you’re dead.”


 
 
The Anatomy of an NPC 
 
Now you know how fighting works, you’ll need 
something to fight against. That’s where NPCs 
(Non-Player Characters) come in. Not every 
NPC will be a combatant, but, when push 
comes to shove, it’s important to know how 
dangerous, devious or deadly your enemies are 
going to be. During Combat, the GM takes on the 
role of and controls the actions of all of the 
NPCs- whether they’re fighting against, or 
alongside, the players. NPCs don’t have full 
Character Sheets like PCs (Player Characters). 
Instead, we keep things simple by using NPC 
‘Stat Blocks’ to describe their capabilities in 
Combat. That way, the GM knows only what they 
need to know, and doesn’t get bogged down 
with unnecessary information like the NPC’s 
background in computer science, their favourite 
hair colour or their questionable backlog of 
Steam games. 
 
An NPC’s Stat Block is made up of the following 
elements: 
 
1. Name and Description. Every NPC Stat 
Block will have a generic name and a 
description that outlines, well, what the 
NPC is! 
 
2. Rank: NPCs are ranked across four 
levels- Thralls, Henchmen, Elites and 
Tyrants. The higher an NPC’s Rank, the 
more dangerous they tend to be. Elites 
and Tyrants also have a Rank number. 
 
3. Secondaries: NPCs do not have 
Attributes- instead, they just use 
Secondaries (i.e. Armour, Defence, 
Necromagic, Speed, Will and Wounds). 
These work in exactly the same way as 
Secondaries for the players. 
 
4. Attacks: Any Attacks the NPC can 
make will be listed here. An Attack will 
have a description and an amount of 
D6s to roll when the Attack is made. 
 
5. Necromagic: If an NPC can use 
Necromagic, their Necromagical 
Powers will be described here. 
 
6. Abilities: Any unique Abilities an NPC 
has can be found here, including full 
descriptions of their effects. 
 
Example NPC Stat Block 
 
Frankenroadie 
A Frankenroadie is a construct of dead flesh 
built out of roadies who sadly perished during 
one of their band’s more… explosive live shows. 
These lumbering, leather-clad giants are 
shocked into life with electrodes, copper wire 
and formaldehyde flasks, before being sent 
straight back to work on the tasks that got them 
killed in the first place. A Frankenroadie is 
strong, dependable and covered in stitches. As 
long as you don’t mind the smell, and keep them 
paid, fed and boozed, they’re hella useful to help 
out on the road. 
 
Rank: Thrall 
 
Secondaries

In the Nightside Eclipse 
Exploring the Ruins of Heaven and Hell 
 
In the depths of the Labyrinth, 
Necromusicians face dangers like nothing ever 
seen on Earth. A chasm may yawn before them, 
its walls a churning mass of razorblades and 
barbed wires, giving way to a channel of acid 
and blood far below. A maze of twisting 
corridors, full of cracked mirrors and 
hallucinogenic fog, may suddenly open out onto 
a frozen moorland, trees and mountain peaks 
encrusted in flakes of human skin. A dark 
factory, rattling with chains, might shift into a 
hellish shrine of infernal runes, which may in 
turn become a ruined academy stage, its 
flickering gel lights and smoke machine casting 
everything in a horrifying neon fugue. Point is, 
that shit is scary. And it’s a big part of your 
Necromusician’s life. 
 
Whether navigating the ruined streets of 
Skin City, picking your way through the acidic 
mud at the bottom of the River Thames, or 
searching for Godflesh in the depths, there will 
be times you need to know how to escape an 
environmental hazard, how to leap, climb and 
clamber away from a demon-possessed watch 
officer, or how to pull a fast one on that 
promoter you swindled in his stolen car. This 
Chapter covers some options for rules for your 
group to use when exploring the Pit, and how to 
use Tests to make them fun.  
 
All of these rules are just that- optional. 
I recommend reading them, seeing what fits you 
best, then going from there. Variety’s the spice 
of life, innit? 
 
 
Why Rules for Exploration Specifically? 
 
 
Broadly speaking, an RPG is made up of 
three pillars (bear with me), namely Conflict, 
Exploration and Interaction. In any given 
session, chances are that you’ll be dealing with 
one or more of these.  
 
Conflict’s simple enough- there’s 
someone, or something, causing a problem and 
you have to fix it. Depending on the RPG, that 
can be a cheeky goblin nicking a sheep who 
needs to be introduced to a mace, or a 
heartbroken man who needs his confidence 
restored, or even something as simple as 
reconciling two estranged friends. Of course, in 
Dead Metal Rhapsody, ‘Conflict’ is smashing 
demons and corpses over the head with a 
flamethrowing guitar. Then setting the corpses 
on fire. Then pissing on the fire. All that stuff, 
about Combat and Necromagic, that’s been 
covered in the previous Chapter, and in the 
Chapter next to come. Moving on. 
 
Interaction is the next simplest to 
explain. Your world- the world of Dead Metal 
Rhapsody- is a world made up of characters. 
You’ll talk to them, share secrets, learn more 
about their lives. You’ll roleplay with each other 
and non-player characters played by your GM; 
you’ll laugh, you might cry, and you’ll hopefully 
lose yourself in the worlds and people you’ve 
created. Interaction, and some useful tips for 
roleplay, is covered in the Chapter after 
‘Necromagic’. Which brings us on to… 
 
Exploration. Exploration is all about 
engaging with the environmental challenges and 
storytelling of the world in which your game is 
set. So much of Dead Metal Rhapsody is 
centred around the existence of the Pit and the 
Labyrinth. The setting, dangerous and 
unpredictable as it is, is a very important part of 
what makes the world fun. Exploring the 
Labyrinth should feel risky and rewarding; 
exploring the ruins of Heaven should be 
horrifying; living in Skin City should be a riot of 
colour, debauchery and heavy metal. The rules 
presented in this section of the rulebook cover 
this pillar of tabletop game design, and answer 
the following questions: 
 
1. How do you, as players, interact 
with and explore the world? 
2. How does the world interact with 
and challenge the players? 
 
Make sense? OK- time to read on… 


 
 
A Note on Encumbrance, Food, Water, 
Heat Exposure, etc. 
 
 
I know. I know that some players really 
enjoy the excessive bookkeeping that comes 
with having to track every little half-kilogram of 
gear you’re carrying, and with working out 
supplies of food and water, and whether you 
covered sufficient distance in the Desert of 
Bum-Fuckery to cause heat exhaustion- and I’m 
not one to tell people what they should and 
shouldn’t enjoy. I find it dull- so do many others. 
But, equally, a lot of people enjoy this stuff, and 
they should be allowed to enjoy it. Their 
opinions are just as valid as mine. 
 
 
That said, Dead Metal Rhapsody is a 
game where you play as undead rockstars 
possessed by the Archdevils of Hell. This is not 
the right sort of game for tracking your last 
remaining millilitres of water; this IS the right 
sort of game for leaping across the Mouth of 
Fulminating Doom on the back of a pair of 
chained sabre-tooth tiger skeletons in a gout of 
blood and sulphur.  
 
 
As such, Dead Metal Rhapsody does 
not use rules for Encumbrance, and does not 
have dedicated survival mechanics. The 
following rules mainly focus on how to handle 
the difficulties and dangers of the Labyrinth in 
fun and snappy ways. What’s important in this 
rulebook is to keep play fast and flowing, the 
anarchic spirit of rock ‘n’ roll at its heart. Sorry if 
that makes it less of a game than you want to 
play- but that’s the sort of game it is. 
 
 
Still with me? Cracking. Let’s go over a 
few methods you can use as GM, and as a 
player, to handle exploring the depths of Hell 
itself… 
 
Under the willow tree, there was a gate. It was 
ringed in iron, and pierced from within by three 
bars of rust and lead. Shivering from the cold, I 
pulled at its bars, and I screamed to whoever 
would listen to let me take shelter from the 
storm. 
 
The gate opened. 
 
And, with trembling steps, I descended towards 
the infernal lights that awaited me below. 
The Simple Method: Using Tests 
 
 
In most circumstances, exploring the 
Pit and the Labyrinth can be handled by making 
Tests. As a reminder, Tests are made using the 
following process: 
 
1. Describe the Action: The player 
describes what they want their 
character to do. 
 
2. Determine Attribute: The GM 
determines which of the character’s 
three Attributes, Guts, Brains or Spine, 
will be used for the Test. 
 
3. Edges, Advantages and 
Disadvantages: If the character has 
any Edges that apply to the Test, or if 
they have any situational Advantages or 
Disadvantages, these are applied now. 
 
4. Roll Dice: The character rolls a number 
of D6 equal to their Attribute that is 
being used for the Test, +1D6 for every 
Edge or Advantage, minus -1D6 for 
every Disadvantage, to a maximum of 
6D6 or a minimum of 1D6. If at least 
one result is a 4+, the Test Succeeds. If 
no results are 4+, the Test Fails. 
 
5. Build Star Power: For every result of 4+ 
beyond the first, the character gains 
one Star Power (to a maximum of three). 
Star Power can be spent for Flourishes, 
bonus effects that apply to the Test’s 
outcome. Any Star Power not spent is 
lost. 
 
6. Determine Outcome: The GM 
describes the result of the Test with the 
help of the player. Test? Completed it, 
mate. 
 
When faced with an environmental 
challenge or danger, the players and the GM 
should work together to decide on a Test, or 
Tests, needed to overcome it. Let’s look at an 
example. 
 
Say the Necromusicians were faced with a 
precipitous climb to the top of a belltower, 
perhaps to recover a sea chest of Heavensgold 
that’s been left there as part of a dead drop. One 
of the Necromusicians volunteers to scale the 
rotting remains of a trellis anchored to the 


 
 
belltower’s side. The GM decides that this will 
require a Guts Test, and imposes a 
Disadvantage due to the rickety and unstable 
nature of the trellis. The Necromusician will thus 
have to roll a successful Guts Test with a -1D6 
penalty in order to reach the top safely. 
 
Examples of Environmental Challenges 
and their Tests 
 
Important Note: Not everything has to 
be a Test! Some of the examples here might 
seem quite trivial, and some might seem 
downright insane. Remember, if failing the Test 
has no meaningful consequences, don’t bother 
making it a Test at all! 
There is also no cap on the number of 
Advantages and/or Disadvantages a Test can 
impose. If something’s super-easy, it could have 
3 or more Advantages applied to it- like taking 
candy from a very stupid baby. If something’s 
horrendously difficult, it could instead have 3 or 
more Disadvantages, such as trying to pluck a 
feather from a Seraphim’s wing. Hey, it’s your 
funeral. 
 
 
Example: Ben, playing as Orlo 
Skulldaggerz, is trying to smash a caravan door 
to flinders. The door is wet, fragile and already 
has a big dent in the centre- it’s not going to take 
much to break it down. In most cases, it’s 
enough to say that Orlo just busts down the 
door- it’s unlikely to be much of an obstacle to 
him, and rolling the dice would only waste 
everyone’s time. 
 
However, imagine a situation where 
Orlo needs to break into the caravan before a 
Sludge Demon catches up with him, and drags 
him off with its rubbery tendrils into a pool of 
slime. Suddenly, that flimsy caravan door has 
stakes attached. Given that, if Orlo somehow 
fails he might suffer a gruesome fate, it’s now 
appropriate for him to roll a Test to see if he can 
bust his way inside. 
 
 
 
Another Example: Tom, playing as 
Marc ‘Cadaver’ Cadwallader, is thumbing 
through a set of scrolls written in Enochian, the 
language of the angels. Very few, if any, sane 
undead are capable of parsing Enochian; the 
living often burst into sudden flame, consumed 
by its divine madness. Marc has no particular 
skill or experience with the language- as such, 
it’s enough to say that Marc cannot attempt to 
translate it, and thus doesn’t need to roll a Test 
at all. 
 
By contrast, let’s say Marc has a slim 
understanding of some of the heavenly runes, 
and needs to decipher the text to save the life of 
his dying friend. Unbeknownst to him, the text 
contains arcane spells that can reknit flesh and 
salve the spirit- if the reader’s mind can take the 
strain! Suddenly, there are stakes, and if there 
are stakes, there needs to be a Test, even if it’s 
very unlikely that Marc succeeds. 
 
Guts 
 
Very Easy (+2 Advantages) 
- 
Clambering over a loose pile of rubble 
- 
Forcing a flimsy wooden door 
- 
Weaving through rhythmic jets of steam 
- 
Jumping over a small crevasse 
- 
Lifting fallen debris 
 
Easy (+1 Advantage) 
- 
Scaling an uneven, but craggy, cliff 
- 
Smashing through a dry wall 
- 
Dodging a flow of gooey acid 
- 
Outrunning someone less fit than you 
- 
Hauling a heavy crate to a show 
 
Average (No Advantages or Disadvantages) 
- 
Breaking down a sturdy door 
- 
Sprinting faster than a pursuing undead 
- 
Dextrously navigating across glacial ice 
- 
Ascending the façade of an old building 
 
Hard (+1 Disadvantage) 
- 
Heaving a great weight off a crushed pal 
- 
Evading semi-automatic gunfire 
- 
Carrying three people on your back 
- 
Climbing a vast, floating ruin 
- 
Battering through a swirling mosh pit 
 
Very Hard (+2 Disadvantages) 
- 
Beating a giant in an arm wrestle 
- 
Surviving exposure to lava 
- 
Breaking stone with your bare hands 
- 
Outpacing an Olympic sprinter 
- 
Throwing an anvil. Like, a whole-ass 
anvil

The groupie boys are less interested in a girl 
who’s all bones. 
Still, it’s got its advantages. 
Long as I drink my milk, I can outfight all those 
fuckers who call my skull ugly.

-

-

- 
Guessing an angel’s personal password 
(spoiler warning: they’re too insane to 
use tech like a normal person!)

Advantages of the Simple Method 
 
1. Very Easy to Manage: You only ever 
need to come up with one Test and its 
associated Advantages/Disadvantages. 
Nice and quick! 
2. It’s Easy to Understand: Your players 
will be able to quickly grok what you’re 
asking them to do, and why you’re 
asking them to do it. 
 
Disadvantages of the Simple Method 
 
1. Can be Reductive: It can sometimes 
feel oversimplified to just roll one Test 
to do something quite complex. 
2. Can be Less Dramatic: Sometimes, 
boiling down a dangerous chase 
sequence into a Guts Test doesn’t seem 
like enough to keep things interesting. 


 
 
Alternative Method: Using Clocks 
 
 
A ‘clock’ refers to a method of tracking 
escalating tension during a dramatic scene, and 
takes the form of a simple diagram split into 
sections. Your clocks could look like this: 
 
 
 
 
Alternatively, your clocks could look like 
this: 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
In either case, each clock is a measure 
of how much time is left before something really 
good, or something really bad, happens. You 
can use clocks when you’re exploring the 
Labyrinth and encountering dangerous 
situations as a method to ramp up tension and 
suspense- it makes the process a little longer, 
but it may be fun to experiment with how clocks 
can add to the stakes of a scene. 
 
How to Use Clocks 
 
 
Clocks are best used in situations 
where the action is likely to be extended, 
evolving and challenging. As they’re a measure 
of time, clocks aren’t typically useful when 
making a Test that is a one-and-done, like 
punching open a door or lifting a heavy boulder. 
They’re better used in tense situations where 
one Test leads to another, or in situations where 
Test may be time-sensitive and under heavy 
pressure. Here’s some examples. 
 
- 
The PCs are being chased across a 
churning factory floor by a demon made 
out of chains and cleaning fluid. 
- 
The PCs have to solve a complex riddle 
in an unknown language before their 
chamber fills up with poisonous gas. 
- 
The PCs have to scale a particularly tall 
and imposing frozen mountain, covered 
in icy blood and demon droppings. 
- 
The PCs have to play the best damn 
show of their lives whilst being assailed 
by a particularly ornery crowd. 
 
All of these situations are extended. 
meaning they’re going to take more time and 
effort than a normal Test, evolving, meaning the 
parameters for success and failure may be 
constantly changing, and challenging, because 
they all present a variety of dangerous situations 
with consequences for failure. They are all 
appropriate situations in which to use clocks. 
 
 
When using a clock, you first need to 
decide how many clocks to use, and how many 
segments each will comprise. Each clock will 
track a different variable, and the more 
segments a clock has, the more of that variable 
there will be to track. As a rule of thumb, clocks 
should have between three and six segments, 
though they can be larger if needed. If a clock 
would only have two segments, don’t use a 
clock- just make a simple Test instead! 
 
Example: The PCs have to solve a complex 
riddle in an unknown language, whilst the 
chamber around them fills with a poisonous 
gas. Ben, the GM, decides to use three clocks- 
the first will measure the player’s progress on 
translating the text, the second their progress on 
solving the riddle, and the third on the amount of 
poisonous gas in the room! 
 
 
Given the language is Enochian, Ben 
decides that the translation clock should be five 
segments- it’s a tricky task, and may take a fair 
bit of time. For the riddle clock, he assigns three 
segments, as the riddle itself isn’t too complex, 
and for the build-up of poisonous gas, he 
assigns six- it’ll take some time before it gets 
truly deadly. This gives him the following clocks 
to work with: 
 
Translation 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Riddle 
 
 
 
 
 
Poisonous Gas 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
 
 
The next step is to decide how each 
clock will be used. As a rule of thumb, there are 
two types of clock- Progress clocks and Time 
clocks. They’re each used slightly differently, 
but, broadly speaking, the main difference is 
that the former is influenced by the players and 
the latter is influenced by the NPCs and 
environment. 
 
 
A Progress clock measures how close 
the players are to achieving something. 
Whenever a player makes a successful Test that 
relates to the goal of an active Progress clock, 
the GM ticks off (or shades in, or marks, or 
whatevers) one of the segments of the clock. If 
the player(s) making the Test generated any Star 
Power, it can be spent to tick off an extra 
segment for every two Star Power spent. Once a 
Progress clock is fully ticked off, the players 
accomplish its goal. Some situations can cause 
segments on a Progress clock to be unticked- 
this includes interference, enemy actions or just 
plain old fucking it up. 
 
 
Example: Ben decides that both the 
Translation clock and the Riddle clock are going 
to be Progress clocks. The goal of the Translation 
clock is to decipher the Enochian script; once 
it’s fully ticked off, the players will be able to 
start working on the riddle to help them escape 
the trapped chamber. The goal of the Riddle 
clock will be to do exactly that- escape the rising 
clouds of poisonous gas before they choke 
everyone to death! 
 
A Time clock measures how close the 
players are to a hazard triggering, or to some 
other nasty consequence  beyond their control. 
Think of it like a burning fuse- when it burns out, 
something’s going to blow up. Players can make 
Tests to untick segments on a Time clock, but, 
as a rule, the actions of NPCs and changes in 
the environment are the only things that can tick 
off segments of a Time clock. Unless, of course, 
the players spectacularly screw the pooch, and 
make everything worse for themselves. 
 
Example: Ben decides that the 
Poisonous Gas clock is going to be a Time clock. 
Every minute that passes in-game, he decides, 
will cause a segment of the clock to be ticked 
off. This represents the players slowly running 
out of time as the gas clouds billow and fill the 
room with poison! 
 
 
 
 
“Alright then, Davey-boy. Listen 
careful. This is your first run through the 
Labyrinth, and that means you need to know your 
route. Get it in your brain, stick to it, and don’t 
think for a second that your ass is worth shit to 
me if you wander off. Got it? Good. Let’s go 
through it together.” 
 
 
“You start at the brimstone wells- big 
stinking things, bright yellow and all crusty, like 
burn sores you get after a bad tan. You’ll pick up 
the cargo from Big Mike there. He packs it into 
the crates, you get the crates on the forklift, 
and you get to driving. Hang a left out of the 
wells, down the tunnels, follow ‘em on then take 
two rights and another left. Still with me? 
Alright.” 
 
 
“Next bit’s a little tricky. The tunnel’s 
gonna open up, and it’s going to be like you’re 
standing inside someone’s stomach. There’s going 
to be fleshy walls, hairs growing out of gnarly bits 
of skin, puddles of acid and these fuckin’ polyp 
things that WILL try to grab you if you get too 
close. Drive round them- at least three metres, 
that’s how long their tentacles get- and circle 
the room. You’re looking for a spine, heading up. 
Yes, I said a spine. Keep up!” 
 
 
“Once you’re off the spine, you’ll find 
yourself in the sewers. It starts off quite wet 
down there, ‘cause it’s not the actual sewers 
until you see a lantern on the wall that glows 
pink. Pink, you here? The other colours are there 
to fuck with you. If you go into ANY of these 
passages they light, you can kiss your ass 
goodbye. No, I’m not going to tell you what’s in 
‘em. Curiosity kills, Davey. It kills.” 
 
 
“After that, you’ll be at the surface. 
Find the client’s street, and pop it in your sat 
nav. Congrats! You’re now a full-fledged member 
of the Hell Riders Pizza Company. Get to it!” 


 
 
Clocks in Action 
 
What follows is an example scenario of 
how Ben uses his clocks in his game of Dead 
Metal Rhapsody. It is told from the perspective 
of the GM and the players, and will hopefully 
demonstrate how this system works in practice. 
Ready? Here we go… 
 
Ben (as the GM): “There’s a clank as the 
door seals shut behind you. A hiss erupts from 
the walls. You look down in horror, and see foul 
yellow fumes escaping from copper pipes in the 
floor! The door in front of you is sealed. The 
cryptic Enochian script is taunting you. You’ve 
got to be quick- what will you do? 
 
Ben marks a segment of the Poisonous 
Gas clock. The clocks now look like this: 
 
Translation 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Riddle 
 
 
 
 
 
Poisonous Gas

“I’ll try to slow down the flow of gas by melting 
these pipes shut!” 
 
 
Millie (as Vander): “I’ll get to work on 
cracking the code- I’ve seen a bit of Enochian 
before, I think, so that might give me a head-
start.” 
 
 
Ben (as the GM): “Alright. Edric, give me 
a Brains Test with an Advantage for the 
blowtorch attached to your guitar. Milie, give me 
a Brains Test with a Disadvantage- this is 
complicated stuff!” 
 
 
Edric and Millie both roll. Edric fails his 
Test, but Millie succeeds at hers. She also 
generates two Star Power! 
 
 
Ben (as the GM): “Edric, the blowtorch 
seems to make the gas flow faster, not slower- 
the blazing heat also spikes, making it hard to 
concentrate. Sorry, mate- it keeps coming. 
Millie, you, on the other hand, make a solid start 
on the text- you thumb through its pages, and 
you do recognise bits you’ve seen before. Nice 
work!” 
 
Ben marks two segments of the 
Translation Clock, and another segment of the 
Poisonous Gas clock. The clocks now look like 
this:

Edric (as Marcellus): “I’d like to help 
Millie by using a Necromagical Power- my Minor 
Power, “Chorus of the Damned”. It calls upon 
my Archdevil to bestow forbidden knowledge 
unto me- can I use it to bestow that knowledge 
to Millie instead?” 
 
 
Ben (as the GM): “I’ll allow it. Spend the 
Necromagic, and describe how you invoke your 
patron.” 
 
 
Edric describes how, in a thunder of 
tritones and brimstone, his patron whispers foul 
knowledge into Millie’s mind. 
 
 
Ben (as the GM): “Awesome! Edric, roll a 
D6 and add your Brains. That’s how many extra 
dice Millie gets to roll for her next attempt to 
translate the text, thanks to the devil knowledge 
in her head!” 
 
 
Edric rolls, and gets a result of 4. He 
adds his Brains of 2, for a total of 6!. 
 
 
Millie (as Vander): “So, I get to roll 6D6 
this time?” 
 
 
Ben nods. Millie rolls 6D6  and gets 5 
results of 4+! She generates three Star Power- 
the maximum- and spends two on a segment 
and one on the Speed Flourish. 
 
 
Ben (as the GM): “You whirl through the 
words so fast, Millie, it’s as if your hands 
become a blur. The devil’s knowledge burns in 
you- you’re so quick, in fact, that you can get 


 
 
one more Test in before the poison gas gets any 
thicker. Go for it! 
 
Ben marks two more segments of the 
Translation Clock. The clocks now look like this:

Millie rolls again- less dice this time, but 
still gets at least one 4+! Ben marks the last 
segment of the Translation Clock, but also ticks 
up the poison as the clouds continue to billow. 
The clocks now look like this: 
 
Translation 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Riddle

Ben (as the GM): “OK, the translation’s 
done- you can see the riddle, spelled out in front 
of you. But you still have to solve it! The gas is 
still flowing, faster now, rising around you like a 
tide of roiling, yellow fumes. What is your next 
move? Around half your time has run out… 
 
Explanation of the Example 
 
 
Hopefully, this example shows how 
clocks can be used to build tension and 
structure environmental challenges in a more 
concrete way. Clocks are great for players who 
like to focus more on the ‘game’ part of a 
roleplaying game, or who prefer things to be 
more concrete rather than abstract. Sometimes, 
it’s nice to know exactly what you’ll need to 
succeed, after all! 
 
 
Some good rules of thumb for using 
clocks in your games are as follows: 
 
1. Use Clocks with Purpose: not 
everything needs to be tracked with a 
clock- in fact, tracking everything is a 
surefire way to make your games 
predictable, needlessly bookkeep-y and 
ultimately pretty fucking boring. 
Sometimes, a handful of Tests and 
some badass descriptions are enough- 
you don’t always need to get technical! 
Equally, though, clocks can be a great 
way to set a scene and build tension. 
Used in the right way, they can 
enhance, rather than detract from, an 
experience. Just make sure that you’re 
making conscious decisions about 
when and where to use them. 
 
2. Maintain the Fiction: we play
