Purson is even sensate, or whether she has utterly given herself to the 
ferocious animal instincts of the creatures within her. She has no fixed 
abode, unlike the other Kings, and wanders her Circle of Hell in search 
of the isolated, the weak and the vulnerable. Her form is horrifyingly 
stealthy; many have turned onto a dark and empty road only to see her 
faces waiting for them where the shadows give way to light. 
 


 
 
 
Asmodeus, Master of the Factory of Terror  
 
Asmodeus is the King of the Engine of Destruction, and takes 
the form of a biomechanical nightmare of flesh, metal and money. 
Churning smokestacks on his back spill out lungfuls of soot and ash; 
conveyor belts and exposed spines chatter with the grinding of gears 
and bone; pincers, skeletal fingertips and electromagnets reach out to 
grasp deluges of banknotes and coins that belch out of the open wound 
on his stomach. Asmodeus delights in efficiency, profit and exploitation; 
he cares not for his servants as long as their deaths would make him 
money. Avaricious in the extreme, Asmodeus dwells in a nest of 
crushed dreams, land deeds, contracts and bottled blood, sweat and 
tears, feasting on the suffering of those that labour in his factories 
below. Interestingly, he is one of the most lucid Kings of Hell, 
offering open deals to those sensible enough to read the fine print. 
 
 
 
 
 
Vinea, Songstress of the Damned 
 
Vinea is the King of the Legion of the Night, and is deceptive in 
word and deed. She takes the form of a beautiful, androgynous person 
of every gender, her form radiating an unnatural pristineness that 
beguiles onlookers with a perfumed musk of pheromones and 
Necromagical energy. In reality, Vinea is a seething ocean of leeches, 
worms, ticks, mosquitoes and fleas, alive with a ceaseless slurping and 
droning buzz as she surges like a liquid through the corridors of the 
Labyrinth. Her form luxuriates in blood and viscera; within her coils 
lie thousands of entrapped victims, slowly exsanguinated over centuries 
with looks of rapture on their faces, even as the fat black leeches crawl 
down their throats and the insects lay eggs underneath their skin. 
 
 


 
 
 
Balaam, Grinder of a Million Corpses 
 
Balaam is the King of the Way of All Flesh; they take the form 
of a throbbing, shuddering mass of meat, bone and gristle, puckered 
with the skinned faces of all those they have consumed. When they 
speak, the Labyrinth shudders and runs like liquid skin; a thousand 
voices erupt from tortured throats, spitting up phlegm, bile and gore. 
Balaam’s body can contort and twist into grotesque puppets of other 
Archdevils and their demonic slaves; it can compress to half its size, or 
expand like a livid carpet of organs and fingernails, or even take the 
form of the Labyrinth itself. Balaam views themselves as a collective, a 
quasi-lucid consciousness of master and slave bonded together by 
blood. In reality, all within it are nothing more than screaming, 
desperate shadows, half-digested and now hideously malformed into a 
gory mockery of the human image. 
 
 
 
 
 
The Unbound Two 
 
The Unbound Two are feared by the 
Infernal Choirs; their very names have been 
stricken from the Diabolus in Musica, and their 
worship has been forbidden amongst the 
demons of Hell. No mortal, living or undead, 
knows their true names, though echoes of them 
exist throughout history. They were the 
primogenitors of Original Sin, the fomenters of 
discontent in the Angels of Heaven, the 
creatures of air and darkness that caused 
humanity to fall. They are Satanas and Lucifer, 
the Great Deceiver and the Bringer of Light, and 
to utter their names is to invite eternal 
damnation. 
 
Lucifer was first amongst the Angels to 
turn his back on God. He saw in her the 
madness that was to topple the towers of 
Heaven, and sought to rally the Angels against 
her in anticipation of their slaughter. The only 
Angel that would listen was Satanas, Lucifer’s 
confidant and lover; both watched as God tore 
Heaven apart, and both watched as all the 
Angels that remained there were murdered. And 
they wept, before fleeing the falling ruins of 
Heaven, vowing to never again serve an unjust 
master, and to attempt to prevent humanity’s 
corruption at the hands of the Infernal Choirs. 
 
No-one is sure if Lucifer and Satanas 
still dwell within the depths of the Labyrinth, or if 
they exist somewhere else entirely. They work in 
mysterious and terrifying ways, and rarely 
appear to any they themselves did not seek out. 
There are whispers of some Necromusicians 
daring to serve the Unbound Two. Whether 
these whispers are true or fanciful speculation 
is unknown.


 
 
“Angels? Really, Dahlia? Fucking angels? 
You expect me to believe you lost twenty men, 
the whole shipment, ‘cause some old rumour 
lurched at you out the Labyrinth?” 
 
 
“Tom. I’m telling you straight. What I 
saw- they weren’t no demons. Not no undead 
either, though they smelled it. They was 
chanting. I can still hear the voices, in my head. 
They had halos- the light… Oh, God, the 
light… It made you sick.” 
 
 
“Bullshit, Dahlia. There are no angels. 
We live in Hell, not in Heaven. Get me the rest 
of my shipment. I don’t care if you live or die 
doing it. You don’t get to go home until I get 
paid.” 
 
 
“They’re waiting for me, down there. I 
know it. I can hear them. I would say a prayer, 
but no-one’s listening. Broken wings, and a dead 
tongue. God fell silent. God have mercy.” 
 
 
“Out, Dahlia. Take your ramblings with 
you.” 
 
Angel Corpses- Remnants of the War in 
Heaven 
 
 
At the climax of the War in Heaven, all 
of the angels were slaughtered. That’s right. 
Every. Single. Fucking. Angel. God Herself, 
driven mad and in a frenzy of sorrowful battle 
lust and self-hatred, cut them down in their 
thousands, casting their divine carcasses 
across the ramparts of Her Kingdom. 
Principalities, Dominions, Ophanim, even the 
six-winged Seraphs of the Holy Throne- all are 
dead, and all are rotting. But whilst God 
endures, so too can the angels never truly die. 
The machinery of death is broken. And so the 
corpses of the angels continue to serve God in 
undeath as they did in life. 
 
 
Angels are disturbing to look upon. 
Once capable of taking any form, beatific or 
even human, they are now rotting in their true 
shapes deep within the ruins of Heaven. There 
are nine Ranks of angel, within three Orders, not 
dissimilar to the hierarchy of the Infernal Choirs. 
 
 The Low Order consists of the Angels, 
Archangels and Principalities. They take the 
form of once-beautiful young men and women, 
clad in rusting golden armour with decaying 
flesh and tattered wings cast in the sickly light of 
their failing halos. Most of the Low Order have 
lost their minds, shackled to the will of the 
almighty, or whatever of it remains. They are 
guardians, protectors and keepers: watchers 
over the dust, Heavensgold and Godflesh that is 
scattered throughout the Labyrinth. 
 
The Middle Order consists of the 
Powers, Virtues and Dominions. The Powers are 
burning words given flesh and form; the Virtues 
are cascading masses of betentacled energy, 
fulminating with the elements; and the 
Dominions are masses of interconnected orbs, 
microcosmic planets and stars, set in constant 
motion around single livid eyes. The Middle 
Order are sentient still, and speak in Enochian, 
the maddening, buzzing tongue of Heaven. 
 
Finally, the High Order consists of the 
Ophanim, Cherubim and the Seraphim. The 
Ophanim are eye-studded bands of necrotic 
flesh revolving around a poisonous fume of light; 
the Cherubim have four wings and four faces, 
ruined messes of animal features smeared 
across human cheeks; and the Seraphim are 
enormous, six-winged creatures chanting 
forever the litanies and prayers of a God that has 
all but forgotten them. 
 
God’s Delusions- Dreams of the Dead 
Creator 
 
 
God is dead. But that which is eternal 
can never die. The self-inflicted deicide of God’s 
tripartite form has left Her dreaming corpses a 
ruin deep within the bowels of the Labyrinth. 
None above know where She lies, nor what 
remains of Her. But those that travel deep 
enough encounter Her dreams. Ruminations, 
idle thoughts, nightmares and delusions given 
form by the impossible power of Her mind. They 
distort the Labyrinth around them into hideous 
parodies of space and time. Reality bends to 
imagination; up is down, sound is taste, the 
sight of a Heavensgold wall becomes the sound 
of amber bells. God’s still in Her Heaven- but, 
around Her, all is wrong with the world. 
 


 
 
 
God’s delusions are impossible beings 
made of the aether- the matter of absence, the 
formless substance of dream. The demons fear 
them, for they conform to no law; the angels 
revile them, for they are blasphemies, 
perversions of God’s once benevolent love for 
the world She created. Very few 
Necromusicians witness one of these creatures 
without going irrevocably insane. Just to lay eyes 
on one is to invite madness, for how does one 
contend with the reality of something that’s so 
perversely unreal? 
 
 
As living dreams, these creatures are all 
but impossible to describe. Picture the last 
nightmare you had, and fuse it with a dreamless 
sleep. Combine that with an idle daydream, a 
phantastic reverie, those little thoughts you 
have when your mind wanders- picture it, taste 
it, touch it lightly then let it go. Whatever these 
things are, they act to protect God’s corpses 
from any that dare venture into the heart of 
Heaven. Perhaps they have wills of their own, or 
are just simple emanations of Her being. 
Perhaps we can only speculate. Perhaps we’ll 
never truly know.
 
The world around her swims like molten flesh, then 
collapses into a kaleidoscope of colours so lurid as to stain 
the corners of the eye. She watches in horror as the 
sound of how acid tastes crawls over her skin. The 
passageway behinds her slopes downwards in time. She 
feels naked, inverted, a perversion in space. Then it 
emerges. Slug-like. It slithers towards her. 
 
The air boils. Its faces split open, and she sees her 
memories reflected in the static. A broken halo over the 
mangled limbs of her old teddy bear. The sensation of 
wings, the feeling of wet redness, a blur of noise like a 
smudge through paint. Its arms reach out, like the 
embrace of a nurse to a newborn child, and she feels 
herself unravel, folding away to her own hysteric laughter. 


 
 
Master of Puppets 
Running a Game of Dead Metal Rhapsody as the Game 
Master (GM) 
 
Architects of the Apocalypse 
 
 
So, what actually is a Game Master 
anyway? What are you expected to do if you’re 
the one your group’s chosen to bear this 
burden? There’s a lot of RPGs out there that 
assume that roleplayers just know how this shit 
works- the players play, and the GM gets on with 
the business of running the game. But, for most 
people, the idea of being a GM isn’t so fucking 
straightforward- and, let’s be honest, it’s not, is 
it? After all, you’ve got to build the world, 
populate it with people, roleplay as all those 
people, know all the rules, come up with an 
exciting story, adapt on the fly to the stupid shit 
your players will always try to pull… It’s no 
wonder that most groups just end up with a 
‘forever GM’, ‘cause taking the plunge and trying 
out GM’ing for the first time is scary. And, hey, 
that’s OK. No-one gets it right 100% of the time. 
The important thing is that you try. 
 
 
This section of Dead Metal Rhapsody is 
designed as a whistlestop guide to GM’ing your 
games. It’s intended for the eyes of the GM only- 
if you’re not intending to run a game, it’s best 
you skip over this part of the rulebook, and have 
a look at the Player’s Aid that comes after it. 
That’ll be more your speed. If you are intending 
to run a game, though, please do take the time 
to read through this material and get to grips 
with what being a GM actually means. In 
addition to an explanation of what you’ll have to 
do to keep Dead Metal Rhapsody running 
smoothly, you’ll find some helpful hints and tips 
to help you be the best GM you can be- whether 
you’re a first-timer or an old hand. Trust me- it’ll 
be helpful. 
 
So, What is a GM Anyway? 
 
 
The GM, or ‘Game Master’, is the player 
responsible for running a game of Dead Metal 
Rhapsody. They set the scene, roleplay as NPCs, 
come up with the core cut-and-thrust of the 
story and world that the other players will 
interact with. Just as you can’t have a game 
without players, you can’t have a game without a 
Game Master- or, at least, not a game of Dead 
Metal Rhapsody. 
 
 
The main responsibilities of a GM are as 
follows: 
 
1. Bringing the World to Life: As a GM, 
you’re responsible for setting the scene 
for your players. You’ll describe the 
environment around them, the people 
and places they encounter, the 
monsters they fight and the dangers 
they risk. You’ll be the one describing 
the stench of a Skin City gutter as they 
search for a missing person; you’ll be 
the one roleplaying as a seedy, sleazy 
barkeeper they shake down for 
information; you’ll be rolling dice for the 
flesh-eating ghoul responsible for the 
kidnapping in the first place as they 
combat it in its lair. Now, that might 
sound like a lot, but imagine it like this: 
you, and you alone, get to bring your 
own vision of the Pit to life! 
 
2. Seeding the Story: Roleplaying is, 
fundamentally, a collaborative 
experience. You work together with your 
players to tell a story of your own 
design, full of hard-rockin’ risk and 
infernal secrets. But, at the end of the 
day, the GM is the one responsible for 
getting the story going and keeping it 
moving. Your story framework could be 
fast-and-loose, or roughly plotted, or 
just a beginning, a big blank space and 
an ending- but, no matter what it is, that 
framework needs to be there. In 
addition, it’s on the GM to gently ‘nudge’ 
the players in the right direction to keep 
the story going and to keep things fun. 


 
 
3. Adjudicating the Rules: You don’t need 
to know everything in this rulebook off 
by heart. That’s a lot of words, and 
we’ve all got better things to spend our 
memory space on than those. You do, 
however, need to have a good grasp of 
the moment-to-moment gameplay to 
keep things flowing. A good rule of 
thumb for GMs is to know the answer to 
common questions off-the-cuff, to be 
able to make spontaneous judgements 
confidently and to have the humility to 
say ‘I don’t know’ and look shit up if 
something confusing comes along. 
That’s what the rulebook is for, after all! 
 
4. Setting Boundaries & Safety Tools: A 
game of Dead Metal Rhapsody may 
involve disturbing or triggering themes. 
It’s up to each playgroup to decide on 
what they’re comfortable with before 
beginning the game- to be self-
regulating adults and respect one 
another’s boundaries and limitations. 
What one player is OK with may be 
something that elicits a negative 
response from another; whilst ALL- and 
I mean ALL- players are responsible for 
looking out for each other, it is a unique 
responsibility of the GM to act as a go-
between and ensure everyone is 
onboard with the themes and ideas 
their game will discuss. This may mean 
using RPG safety tools, such as lines 
and veils, or just having a frank and 
honest conversation with the people 
you’re intending to play with. Either way, 
don’t skimp on this step- it’s more 
important than a lot of people 
appreciate. 
 
5. Making Sure Everyone is Having Fun: 
Ultimately, Dead Metal Rhapsody is a 
game, and a collaborative game at that. 
Whilst it should be challenging and 
rewarding, it should also, 
fundamentally, be fun. After all,  if 
you’re not having fun, what’s the point 
of spending three to four hours of your 
lives playing this game together? The 
GM should recognise when things are 
derailing or becoming less enjoyable, 
and work with their players to make 
them better. This is a hobby, not the 
Olympics- it shouldn’t come at the 
expense of your enjoyment, or at the 
expense of you. 
 
The MOST Important Rule of Being a Game 
Master 
 
With all this talk of responsibilities, it can be 
easy to assume a Game Master is, de facto, in 
charge of the game they’re running. This is NOT 
the case. Nothing ruins a game faster than a GM 
who acts as a dictator. Roleplaying is a 
collaborative experience at its core- players and 
GMs need to work together to make their 
sessions and stories enjoyable for everyone at 
their table. Equally, though, GMs need to have 
the confidence to say ‘no’ where necessary and 
regulate behaviour if it gets out of hand. The 
players can’t run roughshod over the GM and 
the rules whenever they feel like it. It’s a game of 
give-and-take. Be open with each other, set 
clear expectations, read the rules together and 
come up with a game that you’ll all enjoy. 
 
Games are supposed to be fun. That means they 
need to be fun for everyone. 
 
 
Learning the Ropes- What Does a GM 
Even Do? 
 
 
“The hour grows late. Darkness engulfs 
the docks; fog covers the black, silent waters of 
the sunken river. You’ve come to meet your 
contact, Madame Bathory, by the sign of the 
Hook and Anchor. She always had a flair for the 
melodramatic. Fuckin’ Goths, right? Always 
gotta be theatrical. Doesn’t look like she’s here 
yet, though, and it’s unnervingly quiet. What 
would you folks like to do?” 
 
Ben (the GM) finishes setting the scene. 
His players think for a moment. Then Millie 
(playing as Karl) speaks up. 
 
“I’d like to send my Flesh Hound, Rufus, 
to have a sniff under the docks.” 
 
“And I’ll check the nearby warehouses 
and alleyways, in case we’re being watched,” 
adds Edric (playing as Caleb). 
 
“Alright,” Ben replies, “give me a Brains 
Check for Rufus, Millie, and Edric, you can roll 
Caleb a Brains Check with an Advantage, as 
none of the warehouse doors are locked.” 
 
Millie rolls, but gets no 4+ results. Edric 
rolls, and gets two! He uses one for the Speed 
Flourish. 
 
“Alright,” says Ben, again. “Here’s what 
you discover…” 
 


 
 
 
This is an example of just a small part of 
a session of Dead Metal Rhapsody, and 
encapsulates the interaction between the 
players and the GM. The GM acts as the primary 
storyteller, describing the setting and the 
players’ places within it, then reacting to the 
many ways they can interact with that setting. A 
player wants to explore under a loose floorboard 
for hidden loot? It’s on you as a GM to describe 
what they find. A player wants to try and hurl a 
lurching zombie off a cliff? It’s up to you to 
decide what Check they need to roll, and 
whether they get an Advantage for the zombie 
being drunk out of its (admittedly limited) brains. 
You’re a narrator, an actor, and a decision-
maker. But don’t panic if that sounds like a lot- 
it’s easier, and more fun, than it sounds! 
 
- 
GM 101: You set the scene and 
describe how the PCs and NPCs 
change it. 
 
- 
GM 102: You roleplay as anyone who 
isn’t one of the PCs, and facilitate 
interactions between NPCs and 
characters. 
 
- 
GM 103: You decide on the majority of 
rules-based decisions and have the 
final say in case of a dispute. 
 
Here’s another example of GM’ing in action, 
this time centring on a confrontation between 
the players and a rowdy group of NPCs: 
 
Ben (as Craig ‘Cracked-Head’ Carpenter): 
“Oi, you lot are runnin’ into our set! Get your 
arses off the stage!” 
Edric (as Caleb): “Wait your turn, Dead-
Head. We’re not finished yet.” 
Ben: “Caleb, you see Craig’s face grow white 
with anger. He’s a hot-headed man at the best of 
times, and seeing an upstart band like yours 
take his stage is more than he can bear.” 
Edric: “I’d like to try and intimidate him, 
make it so he doesn’t do anything stupid whilst 
we’re playing.” 
Ben: “Alright, have a think about how you’re 
going to do that- in the meantime, Millie, what’s 
Rufus doing?” 
Millie: “Rufus is going to focus on the crowd, 
checking whether they’re still on our side, or if 
they’re getting rowdy.” 
Ben: “Alright, roll me a Brains Check. Back 
to you, Edric. What’s Caleb doing to intimidate 
Craig?” 
Edric: “I’m going to put my foot up on the 
amp, smash out a power chord and use the 
afterburners on my guitar to send a jet of flame 
just close enough to singe his eyebrows. Then, 
I’m gonna give him a wink, and a smile. That 
should keep him down.” 
Ben: “Cool- roll a Spine Check to see how 
confident you come across!” 
Edric: “Two Successes- I’ll use a Badassery 
Flourish and really hammer the point home by 
hurling a crowd surfer in his direction.” 
Millie: “I got one Success- how’s it looking 
out there?” 
Ben: “Pretty good! Caleb’s display of 
intimidation has made Craig back down- but 
he’s not fucking happy about it! The crowd are 
cheering, though. Looks like the club is turning 
around!” 
 
 
As this example, and the previous 
example, shows, GM’ing is a mixture of planning 
and improvisation. In this example, Ben, as the 
GM, set the scene for the players and then 
reacted to their decisions within it. He allowed 
them the freedom they needed to approach the 
situation in the way they preferred, calling for 
rolls where necessary and describing how the 
world around them reacted to their decisions. In 
return, the players engaged with the story, and 
roleplayed as their characters. Ta-da! That’s 
GM’ing. You’re welcome. 
 
 
One, two, they’re coming for you. 
 
The devils are calling your name. 
 
Three, four, they knock at the door. 
 
The devils are calling your name. 
 
Five, six, they’re moving quick. 
 
The devils are up the stairs. 
 
Seven, eight, no escaping your fate. 
 
The devils are calling your name. 
 


 
 
Your First Time as a GM- Getting 
Started 
 
 
So, you’re ready to start playing Dead 
Metal Rhapsody and you’ve been chosen as the 
GM. What now? Where should you start? It can 
be very daunting to GM a game of any RPG, 
especially if you’ve never done so before. What 
follows is a simple step-by-step guide to get you 
up and running, which will hopefully outline 
what you’ll need to know and do to make your 
first game a fun experience for your players. 
After that, it’s all in the practice- keep at it, and 
you’ll keep running. And, when you’re running, I 
promise it’s fun enough to be worth it! 
 
“Oh, my dear sweet baby boy. You’re not going 
anywhere. You stepped into the spider’s web, 
honey. Don’t be surprised when she turns around 
and eats you.” 
- 
Mistress Vanessa Del Bosque 
Arachnotrix of the Black Rose Immortal Club 
 
Step One- Read the Rulebook 
 
It might sound obvious, but a lot of first-
time GMs will jump straight into their first game 
without really understanding what the game is 
all about. They’ll be unfamiliar with the setting, 
or with the rules, or even just the key themes 
and concepts of the game. A big part of GM’ing a 
successful campaign is starting on the right 
foot. If you have a reasonable grasp of the rules, 
theme and setting for the game from the get-go, 
you’ll be best-equipped to hit the ground 
running. So, take the time to skim over this 
rulebook and get your head around  how it all 
works. You don’t need everything, but you need 
enough that, once you start playing, it starts to 
become second nature. 
 
Step Two- Talk to Your Players about the 
Game You Want to Run 
 
Look. You might already have a really 
clear idea about the story you want to tell, but if 
you just go right ahead you’ll be missing a 
crucial difference between roleplaying stories 
and written stories- the importance of the 
players. Before starting any campaign, you need 
to have a chat with your players about the sort of 
game you and they want to play. Be clear about 
your expectations- no one wants to sign up for a 
rags-to-riches struggle for success on the club 
scene, then find out they’re involved in neo-noir 
murder mystery instead. Lay out your rough 
idea, if you have one, and see what your players 
think. Failing that, ask them what they want out 
of the game- they’re the audience, after all! 
Some groups even work collaboratively to build 
a shared setting and background for the GM to 
work off- that can work wonders if you’re 
strapped for ideas. It’s also at this point that 
you’ll have the RPG safety talk with your players. 
Don’t skip it. It’s always worth it. 
 
Step Three- Plan, but Only as Much as You’ll 
Need 
 
For your first session, it’s always worth 
going in with a plan. The easiest way to start off 
is to run a pre-written adventure, such as that 
provided at the end of this rulebook. It lays out 
everything you need to know ahead of time, and 
gives you a nice, easy structure to follow. It’s 
less reliant on your ability to improvise, and 
allows you to get a clear idea of the storyline and 
key events in your head. But I understand if 
you’re keen to get straight into your own stories- 
that’s the beauty of RPGs for a lot of people, 
after all. It’ll just be a little more challenging, is 
all. 
 
Most GMs do one of three forms of 
preparation for a given session: A, a full plan, B, 
a hook-event-end plan, or C, a key beat. Which 
you go with is up to you. Broadly speaking, each 
type of plan looks something like this: 
 
- 
Full Plan: The GM comes up with a 
clear storyline for the session and some 
contingencies in case of anything 
unexpected. Perhaps the players are en 
route to discuss a record deal with a 
demonic mogul, before being 
ambushed by a group of Possessed on a 
Skin City street corner, then fleeing into 
the sewers as they become increasingly 
outnumbered. Perhaps the players then 
arrive at a demonic altar where the 
source of the ambush is revealed… The 
advantage of a Full Plan is that you 
know exactly where the story is going, 
what NPCs and monsters you’ll need, 
and details of any challenges your 
players are up against. The 
disadvantage is that it’s a lot more work, 
and is quite inflexible. 
 
- 
Hook-Event-End: Hook-Event-End 
plans start with a way to get the players 
invested, follow up with a key event or 
story beat, and then reach a desired 


 
 
end point by the end of the session. 
They keep each point brief, and let the 
players figure out the gaps in between. 
Perhaps the players are en route to a 
record label meeting, there’ll be some 
sort of fight or ambush, then a 
desperate escape leading to an altar. 
The advantage of Hook-Event-End is 
that it’s open to flexibility and lets your 
players more actively shape the story. 
The disadvantage is that it’s more 
difficult to cover your bases, leaving you 
with some parts you’re unprepared for. 
 
- 
Key Beat: A Key Beat plan just covers 
the most important thing that needs to 
happen before the end of the session, 
nothing more and nothing less. It 
focuses on the bare essentials of the 
story- the players need to discover the 
demonic altar, for example. The 
advantage of this type of plan is that it’s 
super quick, and super adaptable. The 
disadvantage is that it’s very bare-
bones, and needs a lot of quick thinking 
and improvisation to work. 
 
Once you’ve got your plan, make sure 
you’ve got all the bits and pieces to flesh it out. 
Maybe that includes a map of the area your 
players are going to explore. Maybe that 
includes a list of key NPCs, or enemy stat blocks 
that will be used during a combat you’ve 
planned. Maybe that includes a list of secrets, 
traps or puzzles the players will have to solve. 
The key is to not over-plan- you want enough to 
keep things interesting, but not so much as to 
leave yourself no wiggle room for improvisation. 
Remember, a large part of GM’ing well is being 
able to react to what your players do- if 
everything’s meticulously scripted, you won’t be 
able to adjust as easily when things go off the 
rails. And things always go off the rails when 
players and their wacky undead rockstars get 
involved! 
 
Step Four- Get Stuck In! 
 
It’s a hard thing, to just get on with 
GM’ing your first session. It can be very 
daunting, to present your story and setting to 
your players, and even a little embarrassing to 
have to roleplay as all the different characters 
and enemies they will meet. But the best way to 
start is just that: to start. After you’ve had your 
pre-campaign discussion, after you’ve set out 
your safety tools, after you’ve come up with 
whatever plan suits you best, your best bet is to 
just start playing the game. Rip off the plaster. 
Be willing to make mistakes. Have as much fun 
as you can, because, at the end of the day, that’s 
the point of Dead Metal Rhapsody! 
 
 
But, seriously, I can’t stress this 
enough- once you’re over that first hill, once all 
the expectations and pressure has been 
surmounted, things get a lot easier. You’ll start 
to get into the groove, get more familiar with the 
rules, and, above all, get more familiar with the 
playstyle of your players and of yourself. There’s 
no substitute for experience- be kind to yourself, 
and let yourself learn. And hey, if you fuck it up, 
you’re playing with friends- they should be 
supportive, and help you figure things out. The 
worst they can be is honest! 
 
The world is a beautiful place, and I am no longer 
afraid to die. 
 
Perhaps that’s because I’ve died already. My 
breath is still, and my heart no longer beats. I 
sit at the window of my fourteenth-floor 
apartment, and look out over the ruins of a life I 
once lived. Ashes now. Dust. But somehow, I feel 
better for it. Like I’ve moved on. I turn on the 
light, and explore the wounds in my skin. 
 
I’m stronger now. Stronger than I’ve ever been- 
not in my body, which is consumed by rot, but in 
my head, and in my heart. I think back to before 
the Fall. A quiet café. Her crying face. An empty 
home, pictures and memories, blood in the ink. I 
don’t know if I’m better, but I’m certainly 
different. I know I like the change. 
 
I shoulder my drum cases, and stagger out to the 
lift. The doors slide open, and I climb inside. 
There’s a man waiting for me. He’s got the music 
for tonight. First time on the stage, since it all 
came down. I smile at him. He smiles back. I 
touch his hand lightly, and remind myself. 
 
The world is a beautiful place. And I am no 
longer afraid to die. 


 
 
Key Themes and Concepts in Dead Metal Rhapsody 
When planning your campaigns of Dead Metal Rhapsody, it’s important to be aware of the key themes and 
concepts that underpin the setting. If you keep these in mind when writing your stories, you’ll more easily 
capture the intended tone and feel of the game in your sessions. 
 
I: The World is Metal as Fuck 
 
The world of Dead Metal Rhapsody is unashamedly inspired by hard rock, punk and, most 
importantly, heavy metal. Everything is excessive, over-the-top and dripping in ultraviolence; if it 
sounds ludicrously badass, to the point it’s almost cringey (or full-on, balls-to-the-wall cringe), 
then it’ll fit right in in the depths of the Pit! Picture an 80s metal album cover, add a sprinkling of 
biblical angels and demons, a whole host of undead rockstars and a rebellious attitude. That’s 
the vibe we’re going for. Don’t always take things so seriously- Dead Metal Rhapsody is 
supposed to be anarchic, chaotic and full of energy. You absolutely can tell more serious stories 
(more on that later), but, ultimately, you’re playing as zombie musicians infused with the power 
of Hell. Lean into it! 
 
II: The World is Also Fucked 
 
The world of Dead Metal Rhapsody is also post-apocalyptic. London has fallen into the 
antechamber of Hell, and thousands of its citizens have died, risen from the dead and then were 
enslaved by soul-eating demons hellbent on plundering the wreckage of Heaven. The United 
Kingdom has become a totalitarian religious-fascist regime; most undead in Skin City live in 
poverty, with the constant threat of the demons below itching in their heads. This is the more 
serious aspect of Dead Metal Rhapsody, the tragedy to its black comedy. The Pit is a reminder of 
a terrible catastrophe that changed the world forever; people cope through humour, but for 
many, London’s fall is still an open wound. 
 
III: Live Fast, but Try Not to Die 
 
A Necromusician lives a life on the edge. They enjoy extraordinary powers, but the closer they 
come to Second Death, the closer they come to having their souls devoured by their erstwhile 
masters. They often live recklessly, hedonistically and at full throttle, but always try to stay one 
step ahead of the devil’s due. You should encourage players to embrace this anarchic spirit- if it 
sounds cool as hell, jump right in, but be wary of the dangers of burning out rather than fading 
away… 
 
IV: Horror Grows the Deeper You Go 
 
The average citizen of Skin City has no idea about the truth of the Labyrinth beneath them. Sure, 
they know that Hell has broken open, and that the dead walk again, but the horrors of the buried 
demons and angels are still just that- horrors. It’s very easy for Necromusicians to think they’re 
indestructible, that nothing in the world of Dead Metal Rhapsody can really pose a threat. Good. 
Let them think that. Then remind them the world is so much bigger, scarier and more oppressive 
than they could imagine. The Archdevils, and especially the angels at the heart of the Labyrinth, 
should be considered as terrifying existential threats. Let the Necromusicians be powerful, but, 
when necessary, keep them humble. The world is so much bigger than them, after all. 
 


 
 
Top Five Tips for Being a Good Game Master 
What follows are five top tips for the aspiring Game Master, focusing on the skills we think you’ll need to 
really bring your games to life. Of course, your mileage may vary, but we’d strongly recommend 
considering these tips before starting your first campaign of Dead Metal Rhapsody. 
 
I: Celebrate Your Players- and I Mean ALL Your Players 
 
Playing an RPG is an exciting experience, especially when you’ve spent ages designing a character you’re 
proud of. What makes it significantly less exciting is when your character doesn’t get to shine, or- even 
worse- they’re consistently overshadowed by another member of the group. As a GM, it’s your 
responsibility to let your players do all the cool shit they designed their characters to do, but in a way that 
ensures all of them get their moments in the spotlight. Don’t play favourites, and don’t take away a 
character’s ability to be who they are. Equally, though, don’t let your players take the piss- one player’s 
powergaming can very easily be to another’s detriment, after all! 
 
II: Keep Yourself and Your Players Engaged 
 
Long-time roleplayers will have all experienced that one session. You know the one. It involved lots of 
micromanagement, rolling on tables, little fiddly rules that didn’t really matter- it involved taking away the 
enjoyable part of the game in favour of minutiae no-one realty cared about. When running your games, be 
mindful of this. Is this scene engaging your players? Is it advancing the story? Do you really need to make 
players roll 6D6 times on a random events table, or would it be better to just roleplay their time travelling 
through the Labyrinth’s tunnels? A good rule of thumb is to ask yourself this part of your game is adding to 
the fun for everyone. If the answer’s no, try to streamline it or change it so it is. And remember, you’re also 
an important person to keep things fun for! If you’re not enjoying your own story, change it! 
 
III: Be Willing to Embrace the ‘Rule of Cool’- Within Reason 
 
Say one of your players wants to charge a Dead-Head on the other side of a chasm. So far, so simple. But 
what if they wanted to pirouette over the chasm in a wheel of necromantic fire, before trying to kick the 
Dead-Head so hard in the nadgers he plummets into the abyss below? Sounds awesome, right? But what 
about the rules? Knowing when to allow your players to push things for the sake of them being awesome 
is  a key skill for an aspiring GM. Obviously, don’t let them get away with murder- the rules exist for a 
reason, after all! But do let them try things that are unusual, cool or zany from time to time. It’ll encourage 
them to be creative, and keep them engaged with the game! 
 
IV: Be Firm, But Fair 
 
Unfortunately, disputes between players WILL happen during your games. This is true of any RPG, 
regardless of setting, system or group. Sometimes, there will be an argument and it’ll be on the GM to 
adjudicate. In these situations, it’s important that you’re firm but fair. Listen to both sides, and try to come 
to a middle ground that satisfies all parties concerned. Worst comes to worst, just talk to your players 
openly and act like responsible, self-regulating adults. It’s a game. It’s supposed to be fun. Take a step 
back, reassess your priorities, and come to a positive resolution. 
 
V: Be Flexible, Open and Kind 
 
Sometimes, your players are going to do things you didn’t plan for. Sometimes, things aren’t always going 
to go in your players’ favour. Sometimes, the Necromusicians are going to die- for real, this time. In these 
circumstances, you need to be able to adapt. Keep an open mind, be willing to adjust your story in real-
time, and, if death must come, let it be a good death. As a GM, you are a  facilitator for an enjoyable 
gameplay experience, not a combatant against your players. Always try to remember that, yeah? 


 
 
A Note on Safety Tools and the 
Importance of Session Zero 
 
 
First things first, if you’re one of those 
people that think this part of GM’ing is 
unimportant, get the fuck out. Being considerate 
of those at your table is not ‘woke’, it’s not 
‘political correctness gone mad’, and it’s not a 
‘waste of time’- it’s basic human decency, and 
even in the hellish landscape of the Pit, that shit 
still fuckin’ exists. Are they gone? Good. Let’s 
talk about the importance of Session Zero and 
the safety tools you can use to make sure your 
players are comfortable in your game. 
 
Why Do Safety Tools in Dead Metal 
Rhapsody Matter? 
 
 
Dead Metal Rhapsody is a game that, by 
its nature, deals with themes that may be 
upsetting or triggering for some players. These 
include, but are not limited to: 
 
- 
Depictions of Violence and Gore 
- 
Satanic and Biblical Imagery 
- 
Drug Abuse 
- 
Sexual Content 
- 
Depictions of Trauma 
- 
Themes of Suicide and Self-Harm 
 
Even if these things don’t bother you 
specifically, there’s no guarantee that they won’t 
bother other players at your table. You don’t 
always know what other people have gone 
through, and what they may find upsetting. As a 
GM, therefore, it’s your responsibility to make 
sure a frank discussion is had about these 
topics and their presence in your games before 
you start to play. 
 
 
“Session Zero” is a catch-all term for a 
shorter session that takes place before the start 
of a campaign where you can discuss safety 
tools and campaign content with your players. 
It’s your chance to set clear expectations about 
what your campaign will be about, and what 
potentially triggering topics may be present in it. 
Be honest, be open and be willing to adjust your 
expectations for your players. Don’t be 
judgemental, don’t be resistant to well-
intentioned suggestions and don’t minimise 
anyone’s lived experiences. You’re all here to 
have a good time- that shouldn’t come at the 
expense of anyone else. 
 
What follows is a list of safety tools you can 
use during Session Zero and throughout your 
campaigns of Dead Metal Rhapsody. You don’t 
have to use them all, or any of them, if your 
group agrees. But they are all useful ways to 
ensure your games start fun, and stay fun, 
throughout. 
 
Lines and Veils 
 
 
During Session Zero, players can 
designate certain topics or themes as ‘Lines’ or 
‘Veils’. A topic designated a ‘Line’ is not to be 
included in a campaign. A topic designated a 
‘Veil’ can be included, but shouldn’t be explicitly 
described, as if a metaphorical ‘Veil’ was drawn 
over it whenever it would be explicitly depicted. 
 
Example: During Session Zero, Ben 
requests that animal abuse be a Line and 
depictions of suicide a Veil. As such, the GM for 
the game agrees to not include any 
descriptions- explicit or otherwise- of animal 
abuse in the game, and to not explicitly describe 
suicide during the game, although the topic can 
still be present off-screen. 
 
The X Card 
 
 
The X Card is a metaphorical, or 
sometimes physical, method by which a player 
can indicate they’re uncomfortable with a topic 
and wish for a discussion of it to end. You can 
either give your players a physical X Card to use, 
or designate a signal or word to indicate its use. 
Basically, whenever a player or GM uses the X 
Card, the current scene should stop, the 
discussion should end and the game should 
move on. This helps to avoid potentially 
upsetting or triggering events being prolonged. 
 
 
Example: A conversation about a 
particularly grisly murder is making a player 
uncomfortable. They play their X Card into the 
centre of the table. Seeing this, the GM cuts off 
the scene, ends the discussion and moves on to 
the next story beat. 
 
Debrief 
 
 
At the end of a session, a debrief allows 
players to openly discuss how the session went, 
and feedback openly on any changes they’d 
have made. This lets the GM adapt to their 
needs, and avoid repeating mistakes that cause 
emotional tension. 
 


 
 
Running a Campaign of Dead Metal 
Rhapsody
 
 
So, you want to run a full campaign of 
Dead Metal Rhapsody? That’s the spirit- nothing 
like a heavy, hard-rockin’ saga of necromagic 
and death metal to get the blood pumping! But 
there are a few things to consider when 
arranging your first campaign. Running a 
campaign is significantly different from running 
a one-shot or a short series of connected 
sessions. What follows are some guidelines for 
getting started. 
 
Rule One: Know Your Players 
 
 
When running a campaign, the most 
important thing is to know, and respect, what 
your players are looking for. This is another 
reason why a proper Session Zero is so 
important! There’s no point preparing a 
complex, intricate murder mystery plotline if 
your players are the hack-and-slash type, and 
there’s no point in designing half a hundred 
combat scenarios if your players are mainly 
interested in dynamic roleplay and exploration. 
Equally, though, your players shouldn’t be trying 
to fit square-peg characters into a round hole. If 
you’ve discussed running a murder mystery 
campaign, they shouldn’t be turning up with 
Murder Hobo 9000 and expect to have fun. It’s 
all about setting clear expectations from the 
outset- one, the type of campaign you want to 
run, and two, the type of campaign your players 
want to play. 
 
Example: Ben (the GM) talks to his three players, 
Millie, Edric and Dom, about a campaign he’s 
planning. He wants to run a roleplay-driven, 
rags-to-riches story of a struggling band 
overcoming adversity towards their goal of 
becoming the biggest death metal act in Skin 
City’s history. The players like the idea, but also 
stress they want some exploration and high-
octane combat, not just wall-to-wall 
conversations and NPC interactions. Both the 
GM and the players have set clear expectations-
it's now the GM’s responsibility to react to them. 
 
 
Rule Two: Come Up with an Elevator Pitch 
 
 
There’s a tendency amongst roleplayers 
to come up with an incredibly intricate story 
plan when writing a campaign. This is likely the 
fault of pre-written adventures, and- most often- 
doesn’t work. The starting point for a campaign 
shouldn’t be a twelve-page document- it should 
be a short elevator pitch outlining its story hook, 
key themes and ideas, and the sort of 
experience players are going to have. Imagine 
you’re selling your campaign to an investor. They 
don’t care about the intricacies of the plot on 
page 277- they care about the cut-and-thrust, 
the reasons you give them to get invested. 
 
Example- Ben’s Elevator Pitch: “Cryptophobia” is 
a band down on its luck. Abandoned by their 
exploitative record label, they need to find some 
way to make rent before the Archdevils come 
collecting. A fortunate accident at a dingy club 
show brings them into contact with the Woman 
With the Silver Stare, a demon who, in exchange 
for their service, promises to make them the 
biggest band in history. The story begins with a 
desperate plunge into the Labyrinth- will 
Cryptophobia strike it rich, or will the Woman 
With the Silver Stare sell them out? 
 
 
Rule Three: Plan a Skeleton, Not a Story 
 
 
Campaigns very rarely go according to 
plan. An easy way to frustrate yourself as a GM 
is to have a story all set out that your players 
readily ignore. Instead, it’s best to have a 
defined, attention-grabbing beginning, a 
fleshed-out setting with a handful of goals in the 
middle, and a final confrontation or plot beat at 
the end. That way, it doesn’t matter if the story 
goes off the rails- it’s always aiming towards the 
end goal, has the strong beginning to anchor it, 
and allows for player freedom in the middle. 
Don’t be afraid to steer your players, but never 
let them see your hand on the wheel. A skeleton 
they can flesh out (with your help, of course!) is 
better than a full story that they have no choice 
but to follow. 
 
Example: Ben decides the inciting incident for 
his campaign will be a violent calamity in a club, 
following Cryptophobia’s latest show. During the 
calamity, the players will meet the Woman with 
the Silver Stare, an infamous demon who offers 
them an ultimatum- serve her, and become the 
greatest death metal band who ever lived. He 
decides that the Woman will act as a ‘quest-
giver’ for the players, and that the story will 
eventually culminate in her betraying them, with 
dire consequences…. 


 
 
Rule Four: Give Yourself Room to Breathe, and 
Plan as Much as You Need- No More! 
 
 
So, you have a skeleton for your story, a 
big, attention-grabbing, player-investing 
beginning, followed by a fast-and-loose middle 
and a killer ending your players will all 
remember. But what exactly goes in the middle 
between your strong beginning and end? The 
answer is “enough to keep you and your players 
focused, but not so much as to take away 
improvisation, and not so much that you burn 
out on planning.” What do I mean by this? Well, 
let’s find out… 
 
 
Say your plot hook got your players 
mixed up with a manipulative demon, promising 
them rewards in exchange for carrying out her 
bidding. This gives your ‘middle’ a clear 
structure: carry out tasks for your demonic 
patron, gain more power and learn more about 
exactly what your ‘service’ entails. The exact 
nature of the tasks may vary, but there’s still a 
loose structure there. The idea is to give your 
players a sense of purpose, whilst preserving 
the idea that their choices and input matter. It’s 
a balancing act: enough freedom to keep them 
invested, and enough structure to keep the story 
moving forward towards your defined ending. 
 
 
Equally, you want to avoid 
overpreparing and writing yourself into a corner. 
Whilst your campaign’s beginning and end 
should be scripted, memorable and high-
impact, the middle should be less defined, to 
both save you time and effort but also to allow 
your players to have a meaningful input on how 
the game is played. Use the advice earlier in this 
section on how to plan a session and 
extrapolate that to your campaign. A good rule 
of thumb is to use a set of topic sentences, 
things you’d like to cover in a given session or 
over the course of several sessions. That will 
give you a framework, but avoids suffocating 
yourself with planning and prep. 
 
A Very Important Rule! 
There are some players that claim that player 
freedom supersedes any story the GM is trying 
to tell. They’re wrong. There are also some GMs 
that claim that players should always follow the 
story they’re telling. They’re also wrong. 
Roleplaying is a balance. Players should respect 
the GM’s effort and storytelling, and the GM 
should respect player input and their 
characters. 
 
Rule Five: Feedback and Adaptation 
 
You’ve got a campaign plan, you’ve got 
some sessions laid out, you know where your 
story is going and your players are all onboard. 
Now it’s time to start running the game! 
However, just because you’re playing doesn’t 
mean the campaign planning has come to an 
end. You need to keep yourself open to 
feedback, and be willing to change if the 
campaign’s not going as your players, or you, 
expect. This can be hard- it’s very hard to admit 
mistakes at the best of times, and it’s even 
harder when you’ve put so much effort into 
writing and creating something. But you’re all 
here to have fun- if the fun has stopped, you 
need to understand why and do your best to fix 
it. 
 
It's a good idea to check in with your 
players after each session and see if they 
enjoyed their time. This will help you adapt and 
adjust on the fly; equally, if you have feedback 
for your players, this is a good opportunity to 
share it. Hey, look at that- you’re all self-
regulating adults with the ability to have a 
mature discussion! But, sarcasm aside, don’t 
neglect this. Problems left unaddressed in RPGs 
often fester, getting worse over time. Be open 
with each other, be willing to make changes, and 
even be willing to call it a day if the campaign 
has lost its lustre. Hey, shit happens. Hopefully, 
with the advice in this section, though, it won’t 
happen to you. 
 
Running a campaign is an incredibly rewarding 
experience- when it all goes right. There’s a lot at play 
with RPGs, if you’ll pardon the shitty pun- people get 
attached to their characters, they get upset at certain 
rules or events, they sometimes throw their toys out the 
pram or don’t like how the game’s progressing. Y’know, 
normal human stuff. By following these tips, and by acting 
like a reasonable adult, you can avoid these issues. And, 
hey, if you do, you then get the best part of playing an 
RPG. People are engaged and care about your story; 
players are having fun and look forward to each session 
you’re running; there’s a sense of enjoyment that very 
few other games can ever get close to, and that’s 
something to be celebrated and cherished. So, do your 
best, mate. It’s tough. But, if you get it right, it’s one 
of the best highs you can get. Break a leg, eh? 


 
 
“Y’know, it’s motherfuckers like you that give the 
rest of us a bad name. When I was alive, we had a better 
class of criminal. The East London gentleman. Sure, we’d 
break your knees and throw you in the river if you crossed 
us, but we were equal opportunity murderers. None of 
this… this ‘orrible shit. You… you disgust me.” 
 
She takes a drag of her cigar as the Frankenroadies lash 
his amps to his chest. They bind him up with heavy iron 
chains, their stitched muscles straining below necrotising 
skin. She nods approvingly, her pallid white face cast in 
the orange glow of burning ash. 
 
“I know this won’t kill ya, but it’s the principle of the 
thing. When you’re down on the bottom of the Thames, 
you’ll have time to think. Reflect on what you said about 
‘er, and about ‘im, and maybe make yourself less of an 
irredeemable arsehole, yeah?” 
 
He spits in her face. She grins. 
 
“Alright, boys. He’s done. Get this racist fuck into the 
water, and make sure he doesn’t come back up.” 


 
 
At the Left Hand of God 
Denizens of the Pit and Creatures of the Labyrinth 
 
This section of the Dead Metal Rhapsody 
rulebook is dedicated to all the dark denizens of 
the underworld that may plague the 
Necromusicians as they carve out their heavy 
metal legacies. The Pit, London’s grave, home of 
the undead, yawning chasm of sulphur and ruin, 
is awash with ghouls, ghosts and shambling 
corpses, as well as the unwelcome presence of 
the living. The Labyrinth, the wake of Heaven, 
the tunnels of God’s lament, the ceaseless 
writhe, is even worse. Demons, Archdevils, 
constructs and the damned wander its endless 
halls, searching for blood to spill and lives to 
ruin. There is never a shortage of horrors for your 
Necromusicians to face! 
 
How to Use this Bestiary 
Each entry in this Bestiary covers a type of NPC 
your Necromusicians may face during their 
exploration of the Labyrinth. Typically, only your 
GM should read NPC Stat Blocks, as it would 
rather spoil the surprise should you encounter a 
new and terrifying monster and already know 
about all of its abilities and attacks! Therefore, if 
you’re a player, STOP READING NOW. There’s 
plenty of other things for you to do that won’t 
ruin your GM’s fun. So… fuck off? Yeah? Thanks. 
 
Welcome, GMs- hopefully you’re the only ones 
reading this now- to your little box of horrors! 
These NPCs are juiced up and good to go to 
populate your world and to provide deadly 
Combat encounters for your players. Each Stat 
Block contains all you’ll need to know to 
incorporate an NPC into your games. As a 
reminder, the anatomy of a Stat Block is as 
follows: 
 
1. Name and Description. Every NPC Stat 
Block will have a generic name and a 
description that outlines, well, what the 
NPC is! 
 
2. Rank: NPCs are ranked across four 
levels- Thralls, Henchmen, Elites and 
Tyrants. The higher an NPC’s Rank, the 
more dangerous they tend to be. 
3. Secondaries: NPCs do not have 
Attributes- instead, they just use 
Secondaries (i.e. Armour, Defence, 
Necromagic, Speed, Will and Wounds). 
These work in exactly the same way as 
Secondaries for the players. 
 
4. Attacks: Any Attacks the NPC can 
make will be listed here. An Attack will 
have a description and an amount of 
D6s to roll when the Attack is made. 
 
5. Necromagic: If an NPC can use 
Necromagic, their Necromagical 
Powers will be described here. 
 
6. Abilities: Any unique Abilities an NPC 
has can be found here, including full 
descriptions of their effects. 
 
You can, of course, tweak Stat Blocks to suit 
your needs, should you wish- and you can 
always create your own!. Stat Blocks are 
intended as a guideline rather than a hard-and-
fast rule. If you need your Dead Head to sport a 
heavy gatling gun, rather than a shonky little 
knife, by all means- get to sprayin’ and prayin’! 
Just don’t tweak so hard that all sense of identity 
goes out the window. A Dead Head carrying heat 
is acceptable- but an ancient demon bound in 
ethereal chains probably won’t be sporting a 12-
gauge shotgun and a burning cigar. Unless you 
want him to. Or if it would be cool. Fuck. A cigar-
smoking shotgun demon does sound cool, 
actually. 
 
And that’s a good rule of thumb for running 
NPCs. Select those that make the most sense 
for your story, and that would create balanced 
and entertaining Combat encounters, but also 
try to spice things up with a little diversity and 
badassery. The spirit of Dead Metal Rhapsody is 
over-the-top heavy metal carnage. Sometimes, 
you can get away with being subtle. Others, you 
just have to rip off the plaster and have a twelve-
foot deer demon punch a man’s head out 
through his arse. The choice is, as always, yours! 


 
 
Balancing Encounters 
Picking Fights Your Players Will Enjoy 
 
How Do I Balance a Combat Encounter? 
 
Short answer? You probably don’t. At 
least, not right away. As a GM, it can be difficult 
to gauge just how powerful your players actually 
are until you see them in action. What might be 
a deadly challenge for some bands might be a 
walk in the park for others. As such, for your first 
Combat, it’s best to test the waters and see 
where you go from there. Once you have a better 
sense of what your players are capable of, you 
can adjust balance in Combat more easily. 
 
Ranks- What Do They Mean? 
 
An NPC’s Rank, either Thrall, 
Henchman, Elite or Tyrant,  is a rough 
representation of how dangerous that NPC is to 
face. Note the word ‘rough’- some NPCs in a 
given Rank will be more dangerous than others! 
As a rule of thumb,  here’s what each of the 
Ranks roughly equates to in terms of danger 
level: 
 
- 
Thrall: Thralls are the mooks, the rank-
and-file, the expendable chaff that the 
Necromusicians should be able to blast 
straight through. A single Thrall won’t 
pose much of a challenge, if any at all, 
for experienced Necromusicians. Their 
danger comes in numbers, or as a 
‘supporting cast’ for more powerful 
opponents. Around two to three Thralls 
equates to one Necromusician, 
depending on their EXP. 
 
- 
Henchmen: Henchmen are solid, 
dependable and threatening bad guys- 
not so deadly as to blow through a 
Necromusician alone, but deadly 
enough to give them pause.  Around one 
to two Henchmen equates to one 
Necromusician, depending on their 
EXP. 
 
- 
Elites: Elites are where things can get 
lethal. They’re powerful, ‘miniboss’-
esque opponents, often with unique 
abilities and Necromagical Powers. If 
unprepared for, an Elite can do so 
serious damage to a band of 
Necromusicians, and can also be quite 
tough to kill. One Elite equates to two 
Necromusicians, depending on their 
EXP. 
 
- 
Tyrants: Tyrants are the most powerful 
adversaries your players will face. They 
are monstrous, highly lethal threats that 
can easily put your players in the 
ground. One Tyrant equates to four, or 
sometimes more, Necromusicians, 
depending on their EXP, and can be 
considered ‘bosses’ (if you’re feeling 
dramatic). 
 
What Does this Mean for my Combats? 
 
When your players are starting out, it’s 
best to stick to Thralls and Henchmen in 
Combats, with maybe the odd Elite sprinkled in 
here and there. That way, they can get a feel for 
Combat without getting their arses handed to 
them. As the game progresses and the 
Necromusicians earn EXP, you can start 
changing the threat level a bit, either by ramping 
up the number of enemies in a given fight, or by 
adding enemies of higher Rank. 
 
I wish there was a nice, easy formula I 
could give you for encounter difficulty, I wish 
systems like Challenge Rating actually were 
worth a damn. It would make life much easier as 
a GM, but, sadly, these systems do not take into 
account all the variables at play in a TTRPG. 
They fail to acknowledge the environment, the 
players themselves, the NPCs’ unique abilities 
and quirks, the circumstances of the Combat- 
all sorts of things that have a massive bearing on 
how things will shake out. As a GM, think about 
context when planning for your encounters. That 
will help you out in the long run. 
 
That said, there are a few systems you 
can use to help balance encounters, such as… 
 
 
 


 
 
Encounter Dials 
 
Imagine you want to run a Combat in 
which your Necromusicians face off against a 
vast, seething horde of Thrashers boiling out of 
the River Thames. I don’t blame you- that’d be 
sick. At the same time, though, it would be a 
logistical nightmare. You’d have to track every 
Thrasher’s Will and Wounds, work out how they 
all Move and Attack… it’d slow the game down 
to a crawl. Equally, imagine you want to run a 
Combat where your Necromusicians face off 
against an almighty Greater Demon, but they’re 
all hardened badasses with tonnes of EXP under 
their belts. If you let things play out, you risk the 
Combat being extraordinarily underwhelming- 
not exactly a satisfying  boss fight, huh? This is 
where ‘Encounter Dials’ come in. 
 
An Encounter Dial is an imaginary 
switch a GM can flip to make a Combat more 
exciting or more fluid. In essence, it’s the 
process of adjusting Combat difficulty on the fly. 
In the case of the horde of Thrashers, don’t track 
their Will and Wounds- just give them all ONE of 
each, and have them die in satisfying droves to 
fulfil that shambling horde fantasy. In the case 
of the Greater Demon, dial up its Will, Wounds 
and maybe even Damage to make the fight more 
exciting- but be ready to  dial things back if that 
makes things too difficult. 
 
- 
Feel free to change the Stat Block of 
NPCs as Combat progresses. 
 
- 
Use Encounter Dials to make the game 
more fun. 
 
- 
Increase the challenge when an 
encounter needs to be tense, 
dangerous or suspenseful. 
 
- 
Decrease the challenge when an 
encounter needs to be a power fantasy 
or wrap up more quickly. 
 
Environmental Storytelling and Combat 
‘Phases’ 
 
 
All the best Combats use the 
environment to their advantage. What’s more 
fun- fighting in a big empty room, or battling 
through a crowded bazaar full of tables to flip, 
lighting rigs to swing on and open fires to leap 
through? Your Combat arenas are just as 
important as your NPCs. A fight against a 
handful of Dead Heads is a cakewalk; a fight 
against the same enemies on a rickety rope 
bridge whilst rocks are falling down on top of you 
is much harder. Be creative with your 
environmental storytelling. Give your players a 
chance to use the environment to their 
advantage, AND use the environment to 
challenge them. 
 
 
Equally, don’t be afraid to borrow some 
cheeky bits and pieces from video games when 
designing your fights. ‘Phases’, or unique 
sections of Combats where new and 
unexpected things happen, can be a great way 
to jazz things up. Perhaps the boss is struck 
down by a brutal guitar blow, but then splits 
open like an egg to reveal a horrible second 
form. Perhaps the original bandit leader is 
thrown to her death, but her second-in-
command steps up to avenge her. Use ‘Phases’ 
sparingly- they’re best used for special 
encounters or boss fights, but in these contexts 
can be really exciting tools to enhance your 
fights. 
 
Alternative Win Conditions and Combat 
Purpose 
 
 
When designing a Combat, the end 
result doesn’t always have to be ‘and then they 
killed each other, the end’. Sometimes, an 
alternative win condition, an objective to 
achieve beyond just killing, can be a really 
exciting way to mix up Combat. A fight against 
Demonic Legionaries could just be a straight-up 
brawl- but what about a fight where the 
Necromusicians have to deactivate a bulbous 
machine printing new undead slaves for the 
demons’ army? Think about ways your players 
can ‘win’ without just having to chop their way 
through walls of Will and Wounds. And 
remember that there’s always a place for that 
too! 
 
 
Finally, when designing a fight, always 
ask yourself: why does this fight exist? Is it to 
spice up a session with a bit of ultraviolence? Is 
to challenge players to think outside the box? Is 
it a deadly boss fight designed to push them to 
the limit? Design your fights around these 
purposes. That will help you figure out how hard 
to push and what to include. If you can’t think of 
a combat purpose, chances are you don’t need 
a Combat at all! 


 
 
The Living 
Surface Dwellers, Breathers, Explorers and Thrill-Seekers 
 
Surface Dweller 
The living aren’t uncommon in the streets of Skin 
City. Merchants, city slickers, thrill-seekers and 
tourists often brave the journey down into the 
depths of the Pit, accompanied by bodyguards, 
local guides or their own steel and nerves. Most 
undead are willing to tolerate them; they bring 
useful trade goods and news of the outside 
world, as well as spending obscene amounts of 
money on local gigs and shows. That said, it 
would be a stupid Surface Dweller indeed not to 
bring protection down into the dark. Not all 
undead are so welcoming… and the voices of 
the Archdevils are forever tempting.

Merchant Mercenary 
Merchants from the surface world often hire 
protection before venturing down into the Pit. 
Merchant Mercenaries are hard-bitten ex-
soldiers or paramilitary types with some 
experience of combating the dead. They sell 
their services to the highest bidder, working in 
companies or alone to protect and secure the 
merchants’ assets. Some operate out of the UK 
military, whereas others cobble together gear 
from surplus stores or criminal gangs. Whilst 
deadly to humans, Merchant Mercenaries are 
often little more than an inconvenience to 
dedicated undead attackers. Hmm. Shame.

Daredevil Explorer 
Adrenaline junkies, misguided academics, 
fedora-wearing and whip-wielding lunatics that 
fancy themselves the next Indiana Jones… There 
are plenty of Daredevil Explorers that venture 
into the depths of the Labyrinth, necromagic be 
damned. Often, their overconfidence is their 
undoing- how can you expect to survive the 
predation of demons with nothing more than a 
pistol and a sense of derring-do? Some, though, 
do manage to survive. They carve out little 
legends for themselves, and have quite the 
reputation- and just as many scars.

- 
Tally-Ho, Lads!: A Daredevil Explorer 
can evade the hazards of the Labyrinth. 
When exposed to an environmental 
hazard, such as a pool of boiling blood 
or an acidic cloud, the Daredevil 
Explorer can roll a D6. On a roll of 4+, 
they are unharmed by the hazard and 
can immediately make a free Move. 
 
 
 
Death Metal Dabbler 
Necromagic isn’t the sole purview of the 
undead. Sometimes, the living can get in on the 
action too! Death Metal Dabblers are aspiring 
rockstars who read one too many occult books 
when they were teens- now, they’re deep in the 
weeds of the ‘satanic panic’ and messing with 
powers they don’t fully understand. Whether 
using chalk circles, funky candles or whatever 
else demons find amusing enough to respond 
to, Death Metal Dabblers have some degree of 
necromagical power. But absolutely NO degree 
of necromagical self-control. 
 
Rank: Thrall 
 
Secondaries 
 
Armour 
Defence 
Necromagic 
Magical (5+) 
5+ 
2

Wounds 
Average 
2 
2 
 
Attacks 
- 
Guitar Case Clobber: Just whack ‘em 
with something heavy. 1D6, Physical 
 
Necromagic 
- 
One Minor Necromagical Power 
- 
One Major Necromagical Power 
 
Abilities 
- 
Infernal Choir: Death Metal Dabblers 
unwittingly align themselves with an 
Infernal Choir when they start messing 
with magic. Choose one of the seven 
Infernal Choirs when using a Death 
Metal Dabbler(s) in your game. Their 
Necromagical Powers and appearance 
will reflect your choice of Choir, exactly 
like they do when creating a 
Necromusician PC. You get to decide 
what their Powers are, and what they 
do! 
 
- 
Jump in the Fire: Demons are always 
willing to fuel the unwary and the 
foolish. If a Death Metal Dabbler runs 
out of Necromagic, they can choose to 
immediately lose a point of Will or 
Wounds. If they do, they regain D3 
points of Necromagic and go just a little 
bit more insane from demonic backlash 
and blood sacrifice. Y’know. As you do. 
 


 
 
BCSI Infantry Trooper 
The BCSI operate a professional standing army 
cobbled together from the remnants of ‘Great 
Britain’s’ military. BCSI Infantry Troopers are the 
frontline of this fighting force, well-trained and 
expertly drilled in combatting both mundane 
and supernatural threats. Equipped with high-
quality weaponry and organised into five-man 
combat squads, BCSI Infantry Troopers are 
dangerous threats to humans and the undead 
alike. 
 
Rank: Henchman 
 
Secondaries

Defence

- 
MK-49 ‘Angelmaker’ Combat Rifle: A 
refined, well-engineered weapon for 
prosecuting a professional war. 3D6, 
Physical. A BCSI Infantry Trooper that 
does not Move during their Turn may 
Attack twice with this Weapon. 
 
- 
L5-7 ‘Overkill’ Fragmentation 
Grenade: Pull the pin, throw the 
pineapple- blast the fuckers into bits. 
4D6, Physical. Also deals half Damage 
(rounded up) to any targets within a 
small radius around the target. A BCSI 
Infantry Trooper may only make this 
Attack once per Combat. 
 
Necromagic 
- 
None 
 
Abilities 
- 
Combat Squads: BCSI Infantry 
Troopers tend to operate in five-man 
combat squads, their discipline and 
tactical cohesion improving with 
numbers. For every other member of a 
BCSI Infantry Trooper’s squad within 
Line of Sight or radio contact, they gain 
+1 Mental Armour, to a maximum of 2+. 
 
 
 
 
 
Liturgical War Priest 
Hatred for the Undead is common in ‘Great 
Britain’, and in no place more than the 
Theological Church. A collective of religious 
fanatics, zealots and pro-living puritans, the 
Theological Church churns out a particular 
brand of frothing madman known as a Liturgical 
War Priest. These lunatics espouse hateful 
invective against the dead and, sadly, often have 
the strength of arms to back that shit up. 
 
Rank: Henchman 
 
Secondaries 
 
Armour 
Defence

- 
Battle Crozier: Take a standard gold 
hook, hammer some nails into it, then  
take it to a motherfucker. 2D6, Physical 
 
Necromagic 
- 
None 
 
Abilities 
- 
Prayers of Hate: Liturgical War Priests 
often scream prayers of hate and 
violence as they march into battle, 
inspiring their allies to greater heights 
of, well, hate. All of the Liturgical War 
Priest’s allies that can see them roll an 
extra D6 when making Physical Attacks. 
 
- 
Punish the Wicked: Liturgical War 
Priests can be surprisingly resilient, 
simply refusing to die until those they 
consider accursed are punished. When 
a Liturgical War Priest is reduced to 0 
Wounds, they roll a number of D6 equal 
to their remaining Will. If they roll at 
least one 6, they immediately recover 
one Wound and continue fighting! 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
 
Nomad Arcano-Scavenger 
Arcano-Scavengers are deranged hermits 
wandering the Pit in search of their Mad Max 
ideal lifestyle. Uniformly dirty and profoundly 
mad, they collect magical relics from the ruins 
and bodge them together with as much 
dieselpunk technology as they can get their 
hands on. The horrifying thing is that, 
sometimes, this shit actually works, and the 
devices they create can actually prove to be 
deadly weapons or useful in some other way. 
 
Rank: Henchman 
 
Secondaries

Defence

- 
Nomad Spear-Stave: It sounds fancier 
than it is. Basically just a big bladed 
stick. 1D6, Physical. 
 
Abilities 
- 
Techno-Alchemy: Nomad Arcano-
Scavengers often combine esoteric 
devices together to create ersatz 
combinations of the magical and 
mundane. On their Turn, as an Action, a 
Nomad Arcano-Scavenger can use an 
Esoteric Device. If they do, roll 1D6 on 
the following chart: 
 
- 
1: It’s a Dud!. No additional effect. 
- 
2: Field Repair Core. A target within 
line of sight regains 1 lost Wound. 
- 
3: Soothing Music. A target within line 
of sight regains 1 lost Will. 
- 
4: Necromagical Surge. A target within 
line of sight regains 1 lost Necromagic. 
- 
5: Bouncing Bombs. 3D6, Magical. This 
Attack targets all enemies within a 
small blast radius around the target, 
and can demolish terrain. 
- 
6: Actual Magic! The Arcano-Scavenger 
copies a Minor Necromagical Power 
from a target in their line of sight, and 
may immediately use it without 
spending any Necromagic. 
 
 
Ashgrove Company Lead-Head 
The Ashgrove Company are one of the leading 
paramilitary contractors operating out of the 
wreckage of the UK. Their soldiers, 
affectionately dubbed ‘Lead-Heads’ are 
disciplined, well-trained and armed to the 
fucking teeth. Whilst they’d never admit it, the 
Ashgrove Company often raid BCSI supply 
convoys for weapons and supplies, arming 
themselves with the former and distributing the 
latter to nomad communities and the undead o 
the Pit. 
 
Rank: Henchman

LV-05 Combat Shotgun: A brutal slab 
of laminate black metal that fires brutal 
salvoes of fragmented black metal. 
2D6, Physical. This Attack rolls an extra 
2D6 if the target is within close 
proximity to the Lead-Head. This Attack 
hits all targets (friend and foe!) within a 
cone in front of the Lead-Head. 
 
- 
Hunting Knife: Stab stab stab. 2D6, 
Physical. 
 
Abilities 
- 
One-Two Punch: Lead-Heads work well 
with each other, synchronising their 
attacks to deliver the most damage 
possible. If a Lead-Head inflicts 
Damage to an enemy, one other Lead-
Head within line of sight to that enemy 
may roll a D6. If it is equal to or less 
than their remaining Will, or a natural 1, 
they may immediately make an Attack 
against the same target, as if it were 
their Turn. This Attack suffers a 
Disadvantage, and cannot trigger 
further instances of One-Two Punch. 
 
 
 


 
 
BCSI Paladin Commandant 
The soldiers of the BCSI practice a fanatical new 
form of Christianity, believing it a shield in their 
war against Necromagic and the dead. The truth 
is that God whispers Her insane mutterings into 
the minds of cardinals, bishops and preachers 
alike, unhinging their minds and sending them 
into paroxysms of blind faith that ultimately 
mean nothing. However, through these 
whispers, God’s divine power occasionally leaks 
into a mortal mind. These soldiers, imbued with 
holy energy, are known as Paladins, and are 
responsible for leading BCSI combat units 
against the denizens of the Pit. Standing over 
eight feet tall and shimmering with golden light, 
these Paladins are as devoted as they are 
consumed by lunacy- idiosyncratic, often 
unstable, but utterly committed to their cause. 
 
Rank: Elite (5) 
 
Secondaries 
 
Armour 
Defence 
Necromagic 
Physical (4+) 
3+ 
0

Will 
Wounds 
Slow 
N/A 
9 
 
Attacks 
- 
MK-62 ‘Godhammer’ Combat 
Shotgun: A brutal high-calibre weapon 
with enough kickback to shatter a 
concrete breezeblock. 4D6, Physical. A 
target Damaged by this Attack is thrown 
backwards and knocked to the ground. 
 
- 
Imbued Fists: The bodies of Paladin 
Commandants are imbued with 
esoteric energy, released in bursts 
through their gauntleted fists. 5D6, 
Physical. If this Attack inflicts at least 
one point of Damage, the Paladin 
Commandant may make another 
Attack with this Weapon, rolling one 
less D6 (to a minimum of 1D6). There is 
no limit to how many extra Attacks this 
can trigger! 
 
- 
Spear of Heaven: The Paladin spills 
their own blood, forging its gold and 
crimson fluid into a spear of pure 
radiance. 4D6, Magical. Against 
Demons or Undead, this Attack inflicts 
+1 Damage for every result equal to or 
higher than the target’s Defence beyond 
the first. 
 
Necromagic 
- 
None 
 
Abilities 
- 
God-Sent Avenger: Paladin 
Commandants hear the whispers of 
God Herself. This drives them utterly 
insane, but their minds are also 
completely inured to mental invasion. A 
Paladin Commandant does not have a 
Will score, and can never lose or gain 
points of Will. In addition, a Paladin 
Commandant can never have their 
mind influenced by illusions, brain 
control or other similar effects- they are 
just too fervent for such petty 
distractions to matter! 
 
- 
Symbol of the Realm: The BCSI view 
Paladins as a symbol of their faith and 
of their power. Members of the BSCI 
military, or of the Theological Church, 
cannot lose Will whilst a Paladin 
Commandant is within their Line of 
Sight. 
 
 
 
For the Glory of the Lady 
 
All Bow Before Her 
Righteous Gaze 
 
For Hers is Forever the 
Kingdom 
 
And We Shall Hold Back 
the Dark 


 
 
Demon-Possessed Warlock 
Warlocks are those rare living necromancers 
who manage to enter into contracts with the 
Archdevils of the Pit. Normally, the squishy and 
vulnerable flesh of the living is no suitable host 
for infernal power- nor are their minds strong 
enough to avoid slipping into madness upon 
witnessing the realities of Heaven and Hell. 
However, those Warlocks that do survive the 
process end up bound in flesh, mind and soul 
with a demonic entity slaved to their infernal 
patron. Their forms mutated almost beyond 
recognition, Demon-Possessed Warlocks are 
able to wield considerable Necromagical power- 
and are strong as fuck to boot! 
 
Rank: Elite (4) 
 
Secondaries 
 
Armour 
Defence 
Necromagic

Mental (4+) 
5+ 
8

Will 
Wounds 
Average
